# Work Agents: 

# Systems, Capabilities, and Optimization

§ Awesome Work Agents |  Project Homepage 

## Abstract 

As foundation models evolve from information generation to digital work execution, agents are increasingly capable of completing complex tasks across tools, applications, and workflows. How-ever, existing research lacks a unified perspective on how agents reliably accomplish delegated work. To address this gap, we present a work-centric, system-level perspective that views work agents as systems combining foundation models and runtime harnesses to execute tasks in state-ful workspaces and deliver verifiable outcomes. We introduce two complementary dimensions, work persistence and work openness, to characterize working conditions, and classify digital work into five forms according to task scope and organization. Building on this framework, we identify five core capability families required for reliable work execution and examine ap-proaches to model optimization, harness optimization, and their joint optimization. We further analyze existing evaluation methods and metrics from the perspective of work outcomes. Finally, we discuss future research directions, including sustained work delegation, adaptation to open environments, and the interaction between work execution and capability improvement. This paper provides a unified framework for understanding, optimizing, and evaluating work agents, supporting the development of systems capable of delivering reliable, verifiable, and practically valuable work outcomes. 

Keywords: Work Agents; Work Persistence and Openness; Agent Capabilities; Agent Optimization

31 Introduction 

Advances in foundation models’ reasoning, planning, and tool-use capabilities are expanding the scope of work that agents can undertake [1]. Their responsibilities increasingly extend beyond short, narrowly scoped tasks to larger assignments that require them to maintain an overall objective, coordinate multiple stages, and carry the work through to completion. Furthermore, recursive self-improvement [2] explores how agents can improve their own models and runtime systems, bringing capability improvement itself into the range of work agents undertake. Humans are already delegating complex productivity tasks to agents across software engineering [3], office work [4, 5], web and computer use [6–8], enterprise operations [9], and scientific research [10]. Frontier agents such as Codex and Claude Code [11, 12] illustrate this shift by supporting multi-day, cross-session work with suitable orchestration and taking on parts of result assessment and feedback-driven revision previously handled by humans. Across these tasks, decisions often depend on evidence in related files, records, or applications and on states established by earlier actions. A work-centric perspective traces these dependencies from the initial request through execution to a verifiable outcome. From this perspective, we introduce the concept of a work agent , defined as a system that uses a foundation model as its decision-making core, relies on a runtime harness to organize environment observations and action execution, and carries out delegated tasks within a stateful digital workspace. The foundation model provides understanding, reasoning, and decision-making capabilities; the harness manages context construction, tool invocation, memory, skills, execution control, and error recovery; and the workspace contains interconnected objects such as files, applications, records, tasks, and other actors. A work agent aims to produce work outcomes that satisfy task requirements, with execution results verified through observable evidence. This perspective connects task objectives, model capabilities, system mechanisms, environment states, and verification, shifting the unit of analysis from isolated model outputs or tool calls to the complete process of accomplishing digital work. Work agents operate under different conditions depending on the tasks they undertake. To characterize these differences, we introduce two conceptually distinct yet complementary dimensions: ❶ work persistence 

and ❷ work openness . Work persistence concerns the conditions under which delegated responsibility can be considered complete, distinguishing work that concludes upon delivery from responsibilities that remain active across multiple stages, feedback cycles, or external events. Work openness describes the extent to which task-relevant objects, information, interfaces, and rules are specified before execution, or must instead be discovered and interpreted during execution. These working conditions impose different demands on an agent’s ability to understand its environment, manage task dependencies, maintain execution state, and verify outcomes. Based on the functional requirements that arise throughout the work process, we organize the core capabilities of work agents into five interrelated families: perception and understanding, reasoning and decision making, planning and coordination, execution and state management, and verification, recovery, and replanning. Together, these capabilities support the complete process of interpreting work requirements, organizing and executing actions, and verifying results, forming an iterative execution cycle driven by environmental feedback. The performance of work agents depends not only on their core capabilities but also on how foundation models and runtime harnesses work together. From an optimization perspective, some capabilities can be learned by models through training data, interaction experience, and feedback, while others can be supported by external mechanisms such as tool interfaces, memory management, and execution control. Improving work agents therefore requires considering model optimization, harness optimization, and their joint optimization. From an evaluation perspective, agent performance is reflected not only in the correctness of answers or individual actions but also in the quality of work artifacts, changes to environment states, workflow completion, and satisfaction of task requirements. Systematic evaluation across these dimensions provides a more comprehensive understanding of the practical performance and reliability of work agents. As agents move from executing bounded tasks toward sustained work delegation, their responsibilities increasingly extend across multiple stages and events. In this setting, agents must maintain continuity despite environmental changes, interruptions, and external feedback, while also leveraging experience accumulated 4during execution to improve their capabilities without disrupting ongoing responsibilities. Work execution and capability improvement thus form two interacting loops, raising new research challenges for the long-term development of work agents. Personal work agents, human digital twins, and organizational work systems provide research settings for sustaining goals between requests, representing a particular person’s judgment, and coordinating responsibilities across roles. Future research must address reliability, environmental adaptation, and outcome verification during sustained execution, while also considering the practical value of work outcomes and the human and computational resources required to produce them. These challenges will shape the transition from completing individual tasks to undertaking more persistent, complex, and valuable digital work. Based on this perspective, the main contributions of this paper are summarized as follows: • A unified, work-centric framework for agent systems. We present a unified perspective centered on digital work, characterizing the structure and execution of work agents through six elements: task, model, harness, workspace, work outcome, and verification. We further describe the scope, organization, and execution conditions of digital work through five work forms and two complementary dimensions, work persistence and work openness. • A systematic account of core capabilities and optimization for reliable work. We identify five core capability families required by work agents and examine their roles under different working conditions. We further examine training environments, data and reward design, model optimization, harness optimization, and their joint optimization, highlighting how model learning and external system support contribute to reliable work execution. • An outcome-oriented perspective on agent evaluation. We systematically analyze existing benchmarks, evaluation metrics, judging methods, and execution environments, examining how they assess work artifact quality, environment-state changes, task completion, and constraint satisfaction. This provides a structured perspective for understanding the practical performance and reliability of work agents. • Future research directions toward sustained work delegation. We explore emerging applications, including personal work agents, human digital twins, and organizational work systems, and discuss key challenges involving persistent responsibility, memory and adaptation, outcome verification, and the value of work in open environments. We further examine the interaction between work execution and capability improvement to outline research directions for next-generation work agents. This paper focuses on digital knowledge work across office productivity, professional services, enterprise operations, software engineering, and scientific research. Section 2 introduces the foundations of work agents, including their system components, working conditions, and core capabilities. Section 3 examines five forms of digital work undertaken by agents. Section 4 examines training environments, model optimization, harness optimization, and their joint optimization. Section 5 covers evaluation benchmarks, methodologies, metrics, and execution environments. Section 6 discusses future applications, technical frontiers, and the interaction between work execution and capability improvement. Section 7 concludes the paper. 

## 2 Foundation of Work Agents 

Section 1 argued that the performance of a system that carries out digital work depends not only on its model but also on its interfaces, the information available to it, the organization of its state, and the verification of its results. Studying such systems therefore requires a common account of what the system consists of, the conditions under which it works, what it must be able to do, and how it differs from related lines of agent research. This section develops this account in four steps. Section 2.1 identifies the elements involved when digital work is delegated to an agent and defines a work agent. Section 2.2 introduces two dimensions of working conditions, work persistence and work openness, which describe when a responsibility may close and how much the agent must find out before and while it acts. Section 2.3 identifies five core capabilities that the work process requires and examines how the two dimensions raise their demands. Section 2.4 compares work agents with long-horizon, computer-use, and office agents. 5Task (T)

Figure 1 A system view of work agents. The model and harness form the work agent within the dashed boundary. The task specifies the objective, constraints, resources, permissions, and completion conditions. The harness supplies context to the model, carries out proposed actions in the stateful workspace, and receives observations. Outcomes include answers, artifacts, state changes, and process outcomes. For ongoing responsibilities, they also include maintained conditions and event responses over an agreed interval. Verification assesses evidence from these outcomes, workspace states, and execution records against task requirements. Programs, humans, or model-based judges apply task-specific criteria. Verification may occur during execution, with feedback informing subsequent decisions, or afterwards to assess the work. 

2.1 Definition of Work Agents 

We begin with the system itself. By digital work we mean work carried out on digital objects such as files, applications, and records, for example updating a report or handling a service request. When a person or an organization hands such work to an agent, three things happen: the requester states what should be done, the agent acts on these objects to do it, and the result is checked against what was asked. We call such work delegated: the agent carries it out on the requester’s behalf and is accountable for the result. We first describe the six elements involved in this process, as shown in Figure 1, then define a work agent in terms of them, and finally describe how the elements interact during execution. 

Task ( T ) specifies the objective, the relevant constraints, the available resources, the permissions, and the conditions for valid completion. For an ongoing responsibility, it also specifies the conditions to maintain, the agreed interval, and the criteria for closure or renewal. 

Model ( M ) serves as the system’s decision-making core. Given context supplied by the harness, it interprets the available information, weighs possible courses of action, and proposes responses or actions [13, 14]. 

Harness ( H) organizes context construction, tool use, memory, skills, execution control, feedback handling, and recovery, mediating between the model and the workspace [14]. 

Workspace ( E) is the stateful environment in which work takes place. It contains files, applications, records, messages, tasks, and other actors. These are linked to one another, and the agent accesses them through interfaces within its permissions. Both the agent’s actions and external events change its state as work proceeds. 

Work Outcome ( O) is the task-relevant result that the agent produces. Outcomes include answers, such as analyses, plans, and executable specifications; artifacts such as documents, spreadsheets, presentations, and code; state changes in applications or business records, such as updated records and sent messages; process outcomes, such as a completed process or the stages, approvals, and handoffs finished so far; and maintained 6conditions and event responses over an agreed interval. One assignment may combine several outcomes, such as an updated workbook, a report, and the corresponding changes to business records, and require them to remain consistent across related artifacts, records, and applications. 

Verification ( V ) assesses work against task requirements. It inspects evidence from answers, workspace states, artifacts, and workflow records (Section 5.2.1). Programs, humans, or model-based judges [15] assess this evidence against task-specific criteria, which may be organized as rules, rubrics, or checklists (Section 5.2.2). The checks cover task completion, correctness, completeness, quality, constraint satisfaction, and intended effects. For example, SWE-bench [16] executes tests against a modified repository, whereas PresentBench [17] applies task-specific checklist items through a model judge. Verification may be performed by the agent during execution (Section 2.3.5) or after execution by the requester or a benchmark (Section 5); its judgments support completion decisions, quality measures, and diagnostic feedback for subsequent work. With these elements, we define a work agent as a system that uses a foundation model as its decision-making core and a harness to organize observations and actions in a stateful workspace. It pursues a delegated work objective under specified constraints, resources, and permissions, and its outcomes are assessed against task requirements through observable evidence. The agent itself consists of the model and the harness, shown as the dashed box in Figure 1; the task, workspace, outcome, and verification define the work for which it is accountable. The definition covers a wide range of tasks. In the minimal case, a question-answering system counts as a work agent when answering is delegated to it and its answers are checked against task requirements. Its harness only assembles the question and the supplied material into context and returns the model’s answer, and its workspace is that material, which the agent reads but does not change. A system that edits workbooks or carries business processes to completion uses the same elements with a fuller harness and a workspace that its actions change (Section 3). The six elements interact in a loop during execution. The harness constructs context from the task, workspace observations, and retained execution information and passes it to the model. The model proposes responses or actions; the harness carries out the actions, receives observations, and uses them with verification feedback to update the context for the next decision. At an assessment point t, execution and verification have distinct roles: 

(T, E 0) M,H

> closed-loop execution

(τ0: t, E t, O t), (1) 

St = V (T, E 0, τ 0: t, E t, O t). (2) Equation (1) summarizes execution by the model–harness system; Equation (2) defines an assessment of its evidence against the task requirements. Here, E0 is the initial workspace state, τ0: t is the observable sequence of actions and feedback up to t, Et is the workspace state at t, and Ot is the outcome produced so far. The assessment St records how the inspected evidence meets the task requirements. Assessment can mark completion or occur during an ongoing responsibility. All work-agent settings share these elements and this loop; what differs is the conditions under which the loop runs, which Section 2.2 examines. 

2.2 Working Conditions 

Across tasks, these conditions differ in two respects: whether the work ends once the agent delivers its result, and how much the agent must find out before and while it acts. We capture these two differences as ❶ work persistence and ❷ work openness. Section 2.2.1 defines work persistence and traces it through existing research, Section 2.2.2 does the same for work openness, and Section 2.2.3 shows that the two dimensions are conceptually distinct, illustrates their four combinations, and relates them to the forms of work examined in Section 3. 

2.2.1 Work Persistence 

The first difference concerns when the agent may consider delegated work finished. In some tasks, the work is finished once the agent delivers its result, such as an answer, a confirmed change, or a tested patch. In others, the agent’s delivery alone does not finish the work: other participants must still assess, approve, or contribute, 7or later events require earlier results to be revisited. Work persistence thus ranges from responsibility that closes at delivery to responsibility that remains open across stages and events. As persistence increases, the agent must carry state across waiting periods and events and must determine at each point which parts of its responsibility are still open. Existing research covers both sides of this range. Responsibility closes at delivery when closure depends only on what the agent itself delivers. In early work on reading comprehension and question answering over text, tables, documents, and charts [18–23], responsibility closes once an answer is returned. Tool-use research, from Toolformer [24] and API-Bank [25] to Gorilla [26], extends the output from text to tool invocation. When an action changes the workspace, the work also requires a confirmed effect: in AndroidWorld [27], a messaging task counts as complete only when the intended message is recorded in the phone’s SMS database. Responsibility can also close at delivery after a sequence of dependent actions. In SWE-bench [16], producing a patch involves localization, editing, and testing; in MINT [28], tool outputs and language feedback across turns guide subsequent actions. On the other side, closure depends on more than the agent’s own delivery: on other participants or on later events. When it depends on other participants, responsibility cannot close until they provide the confirmation or contribution the task requires: τ -bench [29] withholds specified database updates until the user confirms them, τ 2-bench [30] requires the agent to coordinate with a simulated user who carries out part of the work, and TheAgentCompany [31] includes tasks that cannot proceed without information from simulated coworkers. When it depends on later events, earlier results or completed stages may need to be revisited: in OSWorld 2.0 [32], revised budget and vendor constraints arrive through a team chat and require the agent to reconsider affected entries in a purchase-order form. When the task defines an obligation over an agreed interval, the agent remains responsible for responding to such events throughout that interval: E-Commerce Bench [33] spans a simulated year of operational decisions, and VibeLifeBench [34] evaluates proactive and persistent assistance as events change the environment over simulated weeks. Dividing the examples by what closure depends on also separates persistence from task difficulty and execution length. Answering a question can demand harder reasoning than submitting a form for approval, yet responsibility for the answer closes at delivery, whereas the workflow remains open until approval is received. The same holds for execution length, a central concern of long-horizon research (Section 2.4.1). A long run may pass through many dependent steps, yet its responsibility still closes when the result is submitted; an obligation to keep a service within its operating constraints may require only a short run each week, yet its responsibility remains open for a quarter. The two examples sit on opposite sides of the boundary: the first closes once its run delivers a result, whereas the second stays open across many runs until the agreed interval ends. 

2.2.2 Work Openness 

The two examples at the end of Section 2.2.1 illustrate when responsibility may close, but do not specify how much the agent must find out before and while it acts. The long run may operate on a repository whose relevant files are named in the request, or on one whose relevant files it must first locate; the weekly run may read the same fixed metrics each time, or have to trace which components a new incident affects. In some tasks, the task world, that is, the objects, interfaces, rules, and actors relevant to the task, is specified with the request. In others, the agent must establish during execution which objects are relevant and how they are represented and related. It may also need to find out where the relevant state is held, how other actors have changed it, and which actions it is authorized to take. Work openness thus ranges from a predefined task world to one discovered during execution. As openness increases, completing the work depends more on the agent’s ability to build and update its understanding of the task world while acting in it. As with persistence, existing research spans this range, and the examples below are ordered by how much the agent must establish during execution. Early research largely studied predefined task worlds. Reading-comprehension and question-answering tasks supply the passage, table, document, or chart to be analyzed [18– 23], and tool-use studies such as API-Bank [25], Gorilla [26], and BFCL [35] expose an explicit set of tools and interfaces. The agent reasons or acts within a space that has already been defined, and the difficulty lies in using the supplied material and tools correctly rather than in finding them. 8Finding them becomes part of the task in web and computer-use environments. In WebArena [6], the information needed for a task must be obtained by navigating and interacting with functional websites rather than read from the prompt. OSWorld [7] extends the setting to real computer environments, where the agent works across applications and heterogeneous digital objects. In Workspace-Bench [36], the agent must also identify which files are relevant and how they depend on one another, so locating material across a workspace becomes the core of the work. Beyond objects, other environments make the state of the task world, the actions of other actors, and authority part of what must be established. AppWorld [37] spreads the information a task needs across multiple applications, so the agent must determine which applications and records hold it before acting. In 

τ 2-bench [30], a simulated user acts on parts of the shared environment that the agent cannot operate, so the agent must find out from the user what has changed. Authority must be established when the request does not state who may do what. In EntCollabBench [38], instructions describe business goals rather than workflows, and each agent may use only its own department’s tools, so the agent must infer which role is responsible for each step and delegate to it. Applicable permissions may likewise have to be discovered during execution: in a card-unlocking task in τ -Knowledge’s τ -Banking domain [39], the agent must retrieve the relevant policy and inspect the card’s status to determine that a security hold prevents it from unlocking the card and requires it to offer escalation to the security team. 

2.2.3 Combining Persistence and Openness 

Having defined both dimensions, we now consider how they combine. The two dimensions answer different questions, as the two examples at the start of Section 2.2.2 already suggest: openness concerns what the agent must find out, whereas persistence concerns when it may stop. The same workspace change can involve both dimensions: discovering which objects it affects concerns openness, while responsibility that remains open as further changes arrive concerns persistence. The two dimensions are thus conceptually distinct: a setting can be high on one and low on the other. As shown in Figure 2, all four combinations appear in existing research. Question answering (bottom left) combines a predefined task world with responsibility that closes at delivery: the system answers from given material, as in SQuAD [18] and DocVQA [22]. Information seeking (bottom right) asks the agent to discover the relevant material during execution, yet responsibility still closes at delivery, as in GAIA [40], whose questions require finding a source on the web, and Workspace-Bench [36]. Service workflows (top left) keep the task world predefined, yet responsibility remains open while the requested updates await user confirmation, as in τ -bench [29], where the tools, domain policy, and database are specified in advance. Ongoing work under changing conditions (top right) combines a task world discovered during execution with responsibility that remains open as later events arrive: revised constraints arriving mid-task in OSWorld 2.0 [32] require the agent to find the affected entries, and in VibeLifeBench [34], some changes come without notification, so the agent must re-inspect the environment to discover them. These combinations describe the conditions of work rather than its form. Section 3 distinguishes five work forms by the scope and organization of delegated work, from work at the Conversation Level, which answers questions over supplied material, to work at the Workspace Level, which operates across related objects in a workspace. Persistence and openness characterize the conditions under which each form is carried out: work at the Workflow Level, for example, can run in the predefined task world of τ -bench [29] or require the agent to track a shared state that a simulated user also changes, as in τ 2-bench [30]. Because both dimensions change what the agent must find out and keep track of, they also determine how demanding its core capabilities become, which Section 2.3 examines. 

2.3 Core Capabilities of Work Agents 

Whatever the working conditions of Section 2.2, carrying out the work process of Section 2.1 raises five questions, each answered by one capability that recurs across office applications [4, 5], software repositories [3, 16], enterprise services [9, 29], and professional workspaces [31, 36]: (1) what the work involves: Perception and Understanding interprets the objects and their relationships; (2) what to do next: Reasoning and Decision Making weighs goals, constraints, and evidence to decide how to proceed; (3) how dependent steps and 9Agent Outcome Agent Outcome Agent Outcome Agent Outcome Other participants Later events 

Service workflows Ongoing work Question answering Information seeking

# Work openness Figure 2 Work persistence and work openness as conceptually distinct dimensions of working conditions. Persistence ranges from responsibility that closes at delivery to responsibility that remains open across stages and events; openness ranges from a predefined task world to one discovered during execution. The four scenes show question answering (bottom left), information seeking (bottom right), service workflows (top left), and ongoing work under changing conditions (top right). Supplied packets distinguish the predefined task worlds; scattered material and magnifiers indicate that relevant objects and relationships must be found during execution. The same compact Agent marker appears in every scene. Checks at the terminal end mark responsibility that closes at delivery. Return paths through other participants (top left) or later events (top right) mark responsibility that remains open. The upper scenes include dependencies within bounded tasks as well as obligations extending beyond an initial delivery. 

contributions fit together: Planning and Coordination arranges dependencies and handoffs among steps and participants; (4) what has actually changed: Execution and State Management acts on the workspace through tools and interfaces while recording progress; and (5) whether the results satisfy the requirements: Verification, Recovery, and Replanning confirms outcomes and guides correction. Each capability is a function of the work process that the model, the harness, or their interaction can carry out, and in a simple task several are carried out in one step, as when a question-answering system reads the supplied material and returns a checked answer. These capabilities form a cycle rather than a pipeline. The numbered panels in Figure 3 illustrate a pass through this cycle: Perception and Understanding passes evidence to Reasoning and Decision Making, which passes a chosen approach to Planning and Coordination; the resulting plan guides Execution and State Management in taking actions and recording observations. Verification, Recovery, and Replanning checks both the updated work objects and these execution records. When the current delivery requirements are met, the outcome can be delivered; otherwise, the work continues at the capability where the problem lies (Section 2.3.5). An ongoing responsibility remains active after delivery until its agreed closure conditions are met. Throughout, the capabilities read context and record updates through a shared work context, which holds task requirements with their completion criteria, relevant evidence about objects and links, plan and progress with pending dependencies, and observed state with results and check status. The two dimensions of Section 2.2 raise the demands on this cycle in different ways: persistence extends each capability across stages and events, whereas openness increases what must be discovered. Table 1 lists the resulting demands. 10 3

The following subsections examine the five capabilities in turn, using one task throughout: updating a business report, together with its source workbook and associated slides, when new data arrive. Each subsection describes what the capability does and what it passes on, examines representative work, and states what it provides. To show how the two dimensions raise the demands listed in Table 1, each subsection then considers an extended version of the task: the agent is responsible for keeping the report up to date until it is approved, a data revision arrives during assessment in an upstream workbook that feeds the source workbook, and the request names neither that workbook nor the owner of the revised data. 

2.3.1 Perception and Understanding 

Before acting, an agent must know what the task refers to. Perception and Understanding interprets objects and relationships: it identifies the objects involved in a task, interprets the content and structure of each object, and traces its links to other objects. It produces evidence whose meaning and source can be traced, including where each target of an edit is located, for use by Reasoning and Decision Making. In the report task, a value in the workbook becomes usable evidence only once its header, unit, and reporting period are known, and an edit can be applied only once its target cells or slide element are located. Existing work studies both the structure within an object and the links between objects. The structure within an object tells what a value means and where an edit applies. For meaning, SpreadsheetAgent [41] extracts hierarchical headers and layout cues from local spreadsheet regions, and PPTAgent [5] analyzes the functional types and content structures of reference slides. For location, SpreadsheetLLM [42] uses structural anchors, cell-address mappings, and format aggregation to locate relevant spreadsheet regions, and AutoCodeRover [43] searches program structure to locate issue-relevant classes and methods. The links between objects tell what else a task involves: RepoBench [44] evaluates the retrieval of relevant code across 11 Table 1 How work persistence and work openness raise the demands on the five core capabilities.       

> Capability Higher persistence requires Higher openness requires
> Perception and Understanding Re-perceiving changed state and detecting stale evidence Discovering relevant objects and relationships during execution Reasoning and Decision Making Revisiting earlier decisions when evidence or constraints change Deciding whether to act or to gather more information Planning and Coordination Spanning waiting periods, handoffs, and events Identifying dependencies and participants during execution Execution and State Management Keeping durable state across sessions; avoiding duplicate effects on retry Establishing applicable interfaces, permissions, and authority during execution Verification, Recovery, and Replanning Re-verifying affected results; replanning when prerequisites change Establishing which sources count as evidence

files, and Workspace-Bench [36] requires the agent to connect content spread over several files. Perception and Understanding thus provides an actionable account of the task’s objects and the links between them. In the report task, it links a reported value to its source cells and to the chart or slide that uses it, so that later checks can follow these links. When the revision arrives during assessment, perception must determine which values already cited in the report are affected; to do so, it must locate the upstream workbook that the revision changes. 

2.3.2 Reasoning and Decision Making 

Located evidence does not by itself determine what to do next. Reasoning and Decision Making weighs goals, constraints, and evidence to decide how to proceed among the available alternatives. Goals determine the desired result, and constraints limit acceptable actions; goals and some constraints are supplied with the task, while further constraints may need to be established during execution. Evidence establishes the current situation and accumulates as the work proceeds, and domain knowledge, learned by the model or retrieved, determines how it should be interpreted. The result is a chosen approach, which Planning and Coordination arranges into a plan. In the report task, such reasoning determines whether two figures are comparable, which calculation is appropriate, and whether more information is needed before an edit is made. Existing work studies both domain-guided decisions and the use of evidence gathered during work. For domain-guided decisions, FinQA [21] requires domain knowledge to reason numerically over financial reports, and τ -bench [29] makes domain policies and user requirements constrain tool-use decisions. For evidence gathered during the work, ReAct [13] interleaves reasoning with actions and observations, SheetAgent [45] queries relevant spreadsheet subviews for its planner and uses code-execution results to inform subsequent operations, and CodeAct [46] expresses actions as executable code and feeds interpreter outputs and error messages into subsequent decisions. Reasoning and Decision Making thus provides decisions that can be checked later. To make this possible, the harness records each decision in the shared work context, together with the goal it serves, the constraints that applied, and references to the supporting evidence. Later steps can then tell whether it worked and whether its basis still holds. In the report task, the record of a decision to recompute a growth rate shows which figures were compared and why they were judged comparable. The revision reopens decisions like this one, and the agent must decide whether it can act on the revision directly or must first gather more information. 

2.3.3 Planning and Coordination 

When delegated work involves several dependent actions or contributions from other participants, each decision must be connected to the work that follows. Planning and Coordination arranges the dependencies and handoffs among these actions and contributions. Planning specifies subgoals, required inputs, and the order in which results become available [47–49]; coordination obtains and routes the information, authorization, or artifacts supplied by other participants [50]. The result is a plan, which Execution and State Management 12 carries out. In the report task, the analysis must be completed before the dependent charts are revised, and any information needed from collaborators must be obtained in time. Representative work emphasizes different aspects of planning and coordination. Planning methods make dependencies explicit and revise them as results arrive: Data Interpreter [51] represents workflows as hierarchical dependency graphs, records task results and status, and adjusts affected tasks as execution develops, and Agent S2 [52] updates the remaining subgoals from the original task and new observations after each subgoal concludes. Coordination frameworks make one participant’s output available as another’s input: AutoGen [53] organizes role-based exchanges among agents, tools, and human input, and MetaGPT [54] passes structured artifacts such as requirements and designs through a shared message pool and a subscription mechanism. Planning and Coordination thus provides a plan that records the result each step produces, the inputs needed to produce it, and the steps or participants that will use it, including dependencies that are still pending. In the report task, the record leads from a revised data source directly to the analysis, charts, and slides that must be redone. Because responsibility lasts until approval, the plan must span the assessment period and take in the revision when it arrives, and the owner of the revised data must be identified during execution. 

2.3.4 Execution and State Management 

Plans take effect only when they are applied to the workspace. Execution and State Management takes actions on workspace files, applications, and records through tools and interfaces, receives observations of their state, and records progress. Execution performs operations through code, tool interfaces, or graphical interfaces within the task’s authorized scope; state management tracks the objects and versions involved, completed operations, returned results, and pending items. The two are connected because operations can change workspace state or reveal information that affects the next decision. The results are the artifacts produced and the records kept, which Verification, Recovery, and Replanning checks; in the report task, these are the revised workbook and the list of calculations and dependent edits already completed. To retain the effects and observations on which later decisions depend, existing systems maintain action and state records during execution. SheetCopilot [4] executes spreadsheet operations through an observe– propose–revise–act state machine and updates its action and state records after each successful execution. InfiAgent [55] extends this practice beyond a single run: it stores plans, intermediate artifacts, and tool outputs in a task directory and updates progress markers, so that an interrupted task can be resumed from the record. Execution and State Management thus provides a record that tells which effects can be relied on. The record distinguishes an attempted action , an accepted call , a committed effect , and a verified effect : an attempted action has been issued; an accepted call has been acknowledged by the interface; a committed effect is reflected in the target state; and a verified effect has additionally been checked against the relevant requirement. These states need not coincide, so acceptance of a call alone does not establish task success. An operation whose effect is known to be committed becomes the starting point for the next one, whereas an operation with an uncertain effect remains pending until its state is inspected. In the report task, the saved workbook and its current version are the basis for the next edit, whereas a chart update that may not have been applied stays pending until the slide is checked. Because the work resumes when the revision arrives during assessment, the record must be kept across sessions so that resumed work does not repeat edits already made; before reading the upstream workbook, the agent must also establish that it is permitted to access it. 

2.3.5 Verification, Recovery, and Replanning 

Recorded changes show what has happened but not whether it satisfies the task. Verification, Recovery, and Replanning confirms outcomes and guides correction. Its three functions proceed from assessment to corrective action: verification checks results against task requirements and completion criteria, recovery repairs the objects or operations responsible for a problem, and replanning revises the remaining steps when feedback shows that a prerequisite or condition has changed. We examine representative work for each function in turn. Verification relates each requirement to evidence that can establish whether it has been met. The appropriate evidence depends on the property being checked, whether a benchmark defines the check or the agent runs 13 it itself. Behavior is checked by execution: SWE-bench [16] runs tests against the modified repository, and SpreadsheetBench [56] applies a spreadsheet transformation to several workbook inputs. Claims are checked against external information: CRITIC [57] uses external tools to obtain evidence for critiquing and correcting its own outputs. Presentation is checked on the rendered artifact: DeepPresenter [58] inspects rendered slides to identify issues such as overlap and overflow. The report task needs all three: executing its calculations, checking its claims against their sources, and inspecting its rendered slides. When verification detects a problem, recovery repairs it. Self-Refine [59] revises the current output from generated feedback, whereas Reflexion [60] retains reflections on task feedback to improve the next attempt. In a workspace, a repair must also target the object or operation responsible, such as a wrong formula or an overflowing slide. When the effect of an earlier operation is uncertain, the agent must first inspect the current state to decide whether to retain, repair, or retry it; this matters most for operations, such as sending a message, that produce an additional effect when repeated. Some problems cannot be repaired locally because a prerequisite or condition has changed. Replanning then revises the remaining course of work: it uses the dependencies recorded in the plan (Section 2.3.3) to identify the steps the change affects and draws on Planning and Coordination to rebuild them. LATS [61] searches over alternative reasoning and action paths, using environmental feedback and reflection to choose among them. Verification, Recovery, and Replanning thus provides confirmed outcomes and corrections matched to the scope of each problem: a local defect receives a local repair and recheck, whereas a changed prerequisite leads to a revised plan and a check of its downstream results. After the revision, affected results already checked must be re-verified, and the agent must first establish which source counts as evidence for the revised figures. With it, the cycle in Figure 3 closes: a confirmed result is delivered or becomes the basis for further work, and a remaining problem is directed to the capability it concerns, with missing evidence going back to Perception and Understanding, a questionable decision to Reasoning and Decision Making, a changed prerequisite to Planning and Coordination, and an execution error to Execution and State Management. Section 2.4 relates this framework to existing agent paradigms. 

2.4 Work Agents and Related Agent Paradigms 

Relative to this framework, three established lines of agent research each focus on one facet of the work: long-horizon agents on sustaining execution over many steps, computer-use agents on the action interface, and office agents on the type of artifact produced. A work agent is instead defined by its delegated objective and by outcomes assessed against task requirements, regardless of how long it runs, which interface it uses, or what artifacts it produces. Sections 2.4.1 to 2.4.3 summarize the research focus of each paradigm and state what the work-agent view adds, and Section 2.4.4 shows how the perspectives combine on one shared task. 

2.4.1 Long-Horizon Agents 

The first paradigm concerns sustaining execution over many steps. Long-horizon agents study how to maintain task progress, context, and state over extended execution and how to handle dependencies whose consequences emerge in later stages. A recent synthesis organizes this research into three levels [62]: the intra-context level covers coupled reasoning steps, the cross-context level covers state continuity beyond a single context window, and the cross-task level covers experience accumulation across tasks. Evaluations quantify this ability: the METR time-horizon analysis [63] measures task length by the time human experts take to complete the task and estimates the length at which an agent reaches a given success rate, while RE-Bench [64] compares research-engineering performance under different time budgets. To sustain such work, representative methods keep information available beyond a single context window. InfiAgent [55] constructs its reasoning context from a snapshot of its stored task state and a fixed window of recent actions. On the evaluation side, LongMemEval [65] tests memory through timestamped user–assistant histories, including information extraction, multi-session reasoning, temporal reasoning, and updates to user information, and LoCoMo [66] evaluates very long-term conversational memory. 14 These methods and measures concern continuity of execution and retained information. Work persistence asks a different question: when responsibility may close (Section 2.2.1). Persistent delegation requires tracking open obligations, responding to other participants and later events, and re-verifying earlier results against the current workspace. 

2.4.2 Computer-Use Agents 

The second paradigm concerns the interface through which actions are taken. Computer-use agents study how to complete tasks through software interfaces in browsers, desktop applications, and operating systems. A central problem is to ground task intent in operations executable in the current interface: identifying relevant controls, locating action targets, and deciding what to do next from the resulting interface state [67–71]. SeeClick [72] studies the visual-grounding part of this process by locating screen elements from screenshots and instructions, and uses GUI-grounding pre-training to improve downstream task performance. Grounding individual operations is not enough; the operations must also add up to the task outcome. Bench-marks in this line, such as WebVoyager [73], therefore evaluate whole sequences of operations. WebArena [6] checks, depending on the task, whether an answer, page location, or website state satisfies the intended goal, and OSWorld [7] uses task-specific scripts to check the resulting files and application states. The work-agent view places these interface operations within the delegated task as a whole. Computer use is one interface through which the harness acts on the workspace [74]. Beyond the interface, this view also asks when responsibility closes and which permissions and dependencies the agent must discover during execution, including dependencies on objects that the interface does not show. Relating interface operations to these questions shows what each operation contributes to the task. 

2.4.3 Office Agents 

The third paradigm concerns the type of artifact produced. Office agents, such as SheetCopilot, PPTAgent, and Huawei Cloud OfficeAce [4, 5, 75], create and modify common work artifacts such as documents, spreadsheets, and presentations. Task requirements concern both content and the structure and use of the objects that carry it: spreadsheet operations must act on the correct data and cells, while presentation production must balance content, layout, and overall coherence. The quality of the resulting artifact depends both on how individual objects are manipulated and on how the task is organized. Representative studies address these two aspects. For object manipulation, SheetCopilot [4] controls spread-sheets through a set of atomic actions, and PPTAgent [5] generates slides from reference presentations through code-based editing and evaluates content, design, and coherence. For task organization, OfficeBench [76] provides single- and multi-application tasks involving documents, spreadsheets, calendars, and email; its cross-application tasks require information obtained earlier to be used in later operations, and their results are verified through matching rules or execution-based checks. Office agents thus take office objects and the workflows around them as their main setting. The work-agent view offers a framework for analyzing such work alongside software, service, and organizational work: it describes office outcomes in the same terms as other work outcomes (Section 2.1), so that an updated workbook, the corresponding changes to business records, and the approval required before publication can be analyzed as parts of one delegated task. 

2.4.4 Relationships among Paradigms 

Having examined each paradigm separately, we now compare them on the same task. Table 2 uses the report task from Section 2.3, updating a business report when new data arrive; as its column “What is tracked” shows, the three related paradigms each track a different part of this task. A single system may need all three, as when an office agent completes a long reporting task through desktop applications and must maintain state over many steps. The work-agent view places these concerns within one work process, from task requirements through execution in the workspace to verified outcomes. This allows the paper to relate research across paradigms, identify which parts of the work each supports, and determine which capabilities must still be combined for a given delegated task. 15 Table 2 Work agents and related agent paradigms, compared through the task of updating a business report when new data arrive. These perspectives are complementary and can be combined within one system. 

Perspective Core question What is tracked Same task, different focus 

Long-Horizon Agents How is work sustained over many steps? Progress, prior results, and pending dependencies Resume from prior results and incorporate new data. Computer-Use Agents How are actions grounded in software interfaces? Targets, interface state, and action effects Locate cells, edit values, and confirm the saved state. Office Agents How are office artifacts created and updated? Content, structure, layout, and cross-artifact links Update the workbook, report, and associated slides. Work Agents How is delegated work carried through to a verified outcome? Task requirements, workspace state, progress, outcomes, and evidence Plan the update, integrate new data, revise linked artifacts, and verify the agreed deliverables. 

The next section examines the scope and organization of the work delegated to work agents. 16 3 Work Forms: What Work Do Agents Undertake? 

Section 2 describes the system elements, working conditions, and core capabilities of work agents. Together, these provide a foundation for explaining how an agent interprets a task, organizes its actions, and checks their effects. The demands on these capabilities depend on the work delegated to the system. We examine that work through three questions: what outcome must the agent produce, which objects and dependencies fall within its responsibility, and what conditions determine completion? The five work forms below describe how these responsibilities are organized. Because a workflow can contain informational responses and local actions, Figure 4 assigns each representative task setting to the broadest form it requires. At the Conversation Level, the agent produces an informational result, whereas at the Atomic Action Level, it executes a local operation on a work object. Moving to the Multi-step Level, the agent combines such dependent actions to achieve a complete objective. This responsibility broadens at the Workflow Level, where the process also becomes part of the delegated work and the agent advances it through stages governed by procedural requirements and interactions with other participants. It extends further at the Workspace Level, where the request no longer specifies every object involved and the agent must establish which files, records, and applications bear on the work and how they are related. As the scope expands, the agent must manage more of the dependencies surrounding an outcome, from intermediate results to stage transitions and relationships among work objects. Table 3 summarizes the resulting work requirements. 

3.1 Conversation Level 

Work at the Conversation Level produces an informational result from a request and its associated evidence, such as an answer, analysis, classification, or judgment. The agent interprets the relevant material and applies the knowledge or computation needed to produce that result. Completion is assessed on the returned information and does not require modifying the source artifacts, external work objects, or their states. 

3.1.1 Producing Informational Outcomes 

In these tasks, the delegated deliverable is information that answers the requester’s question or supports a judgment. It may range from a short factual answer to an assessment, explanation, or detailed proposal. SQuAD asks for an answer extracted from a supplied passage [18], FinQA requires a numerical answer derived from financial text and tables [21], and MMTU includes classifications and judgments about whether a statement is supported [77]. Professional requests extend these demands: AAAR-1.0 asks for equation checks and critiques of research papers [78]; FinLFQA requests financial analyses grounded in sources and calculations [79]; PRBench asks for justified legal and financial advice [80]; and MMLongBench includes summarization of supplied PDF documents [81]. Responses can also take the form of plans or executable specifications. In Idea2Plan’s direct-prompting setting, models develop a supplied research idea into a plan that includes experimental design, while TravelPlanner’s sole-planning setting asks for a feasible itinerary based on supplied travel information [82, 83]. WorfBench asks for a workflow graph of proposed subtasks and dependencies [84]. Program outputs specify how an object can be constructed: BlenderLLM’s CADBench asks for Blender scripts from modeling instructions, while the CADBench reconstruction benchmark asks for editable CAD programs from supplied images or meshes [85, 86]. 

3.1.2 Interpreting Evidence and Deriving Responses 

The complexity of a response task depends on both the source material to be understood and the reasoning needed to process it. Prose presents facts in linguistic context, while tables relate values through rows and columns [87, 88]. Document images carry information in page layout [89], while charts encode values and relationships visually [23]. Extracting a value or comparing entries therefore depends on interpreting these structures [21, 22, 90]. In GDP.pdf, a footnote changes which employers belong in a chronological ranking [91]. Repository code and structured data also appear as supplied evidence in LongBench v2’s multiple-choice comprehension tasks [92]. 17 Evolution of Agent Work 2023–2026

Colors follow the five work forms in Section 3 and classify selected task settings, not entire methods or frameworks. Method settings include Generative Agents interviews, Self-Refine FED dialogue, MemGPT MSC dialogue, numerical answers in Toolformer, DSPy, AFlow and Agent Lightning, CRITIC web-evidence QA, AutoGen and Reflexion ALFWorld tasks, Magentic-One GAIA tasks, a specified UI-TARS slide edit, ReasoningBank full purchase-history lookup, and an EnvCraft released literature-synthesis task. Benchmark settings include FinanceBench long context, BFCL single calls, WorkArena forms, PPTC individual edits, MedAgentBench blood-pressure documentation, AndroidWorld SMS, two-stage TravelPlanner, interactive AgentBench, feedback-based MINT, MetaGPT procedures, local GUI action or grounding for CogAgent, GUI-Actor and ScreenSpot-Pro, and BenchCAD numeric QA. Workspace examples use SWE-bench repository repair with SWE-agent and OpenHands, SWE-Lancer individual-contributor tasks, Spider 2.0 DBT projects, CRMArena-Pro records, Toolathlon cross-source work, OfficeQA Pro full-corpus search, GraphForge and WorkForge task synthesis, OdysseyBench history-referenced objects, BankerToolBench data-room discovery, CI-Work tool-based retrieval, and LocAgent repository localization. Terminal-Bench uses 2.0. Years refer to selected public versions; AIOpsLab uses its 2025 paper despite 2024 predecessors. Logos identify recorded first-author or joint-first-author institutions. 

18 The scope of the supplied material determines where relevant evidence must be found, while relationships among its parts determine what must be connected. FinanceBench’s long-context setting supplies the relevant filing in the prompt [93]; multi-hop and long-document tasks require linking supporting information across passages, pages, or documents [19, 94–97]. A synthesis must further combine these contributions into an account that preserves their meaning [79, 81]. ConvFinQA extends the dependency to conversation history, with follow-up calculations using values or results established in earlier turns [98]. The requested inference determines how the evidence is used. A numerical answer may require computing with selected values, sometimes through an intermediate program [21, 99, 100], while a legal conclusion may require applying an explicit rule to the facts [101]. Scientific assessments draw on domain knowledge to interpret the evidence [102, 103]. Plans require comparing available options against stated requirements and commonsense feasibility constraints, including whether the proposed choices can be combined into a workable arrangement [78, 83]. An informational response can explain why a report contains an incorrect value. Correcting that value extends the responsibility to changing the relevant workbook entry and checking that the update has taken effect. 

3.2 Atomic Action Level 

This transition from generating information to modifying state defines the Atomic Action Level, which assigns the agent a local objective with a clearly defined target and effect, such as correcting a spreadsheet cell, repositioning a slide element, or updating a record field. Here, the level is determined by the local scope of the work objective rather than the number of low-level execution steps: a single local action may still require several mouse, keyboard, or API operations to complete. Regardless of the interface mechanics, the agent must apply the change to the intended object while preserving any properties that the request leaves unchanged [104]. 

3.2.1 Creating and Modifying Local Work Objects 

A request may add a bounded element to an existing artifact, such as a formula [105, 106], a conditional formatting rule [107], or a chart in WTM-Bench’s single-artifact tasks [108]. String transformations and spreadsheet data manipulation can likewise be specified through examples or natural language [109, 110]. Existing content can also be edited: InstructExcel expresses spreadsheet operations as scripts, PPTC includes individual edits to slide text, styles, and positions, and MiLDEdit specifies design-layer changes that preserve surrounding layout and non-target content [111–113]. The same local scope applies to application records and communication state. WorkArena’s form tasks request records with specified field values, while WorkBench includes scheduling a particular event and replying to or deleting a selected email [9, 114]. Each request supplies a target and an intended change that execution must realize. 

3.2.2 Executing and Checking a Local Change 

To carry out these requests, the agent must identify the target and specify the operation through an available interface. ScreenSpot-Pro isolates target location in professional application screenshots [115]. BFCL’s single-call tasks examine function and argument specifications; MedAgentBench adds patient-specific parameters through a blood-pressure documentation request linked to the intended record [35, 116]. After execution, completion depends on the resulting state: an artifact must contain the requested content or property, and an application record or message must reflect the intended change. In AndroidWorld’s messaging task, the evaluator checks the destination number and message content in the phone’s SMS database [27]. Inspecting the resulting state during execution can likewise tell the agent whether the operation needs correction. 19 3.3 Multi-step Level 

Many work objectives cannot be met by a single local change: they depend on intermediate results that are not available at the start. Preparing a forecast, for example, requires importing data before constructing calculations and checking the resulting workbook. Intermediate operations establish information or state on which later decisions depend. Work at the Multi-step Level coordinates these dependent operations toward a complete objective, adapting subsequent actions to intermediate results and execution feedback [117]. Because only the objective is delegated, completion is assessed on the final result, whichever sequence of actions reaches it. 

3.3.1 Completing Objectives through Intermediate Results 

A complete objective may culminate in a usable artifact, an informational result assembled from acquired evidence, or a change to an operational environment. Intermediate results supply the components and inputs needed to complete that objective. In data-science tasks, data preparation and code execution lead to an executable analysis, as illustrated by DataSciBench and ScienceAgentBench [10, 118]. Artifact production also requires the parts to work together. Slide generation connects content selection and visual planning [119, 120]; each slide must fit the deck’s outline and surrounding narrative [5, 17]. In a financial workbook, a value computed on one worksheet may supply calculations on another [121]. Completion therefore depends on both the individual transformations and the relationships among their results. Intermediate results can also supply the evidence for an answer or plan. TravelPlanner’s two-stage setting requires collecting travel options through tools before assembling an itinerary that satisfies the user’s constraints [83]. GAIA includes questions that require locating a study on the web before recovering the requested fact [40]. For tasks that change an environment, intermediate changes establish the state needed to complete the requested operation. Terminal-Bench 2.0 includes configuring systems and migrating databases [122]. In AIOpsLab’s mitigation tasks, the objective is to restore a running service through interventions such as configuration changes or version rollbacks [123]. Desktop and web tasks carry these intermediate results across applications. In OSWorld, payment records determine the recipients of a reminder email; WebArena’s museum-itinerary example combines reference pages and a map before recording the route in a project README [6, 7]. 

3.3.2 Managing Action Dependencies and Feedback 

These dependencies shape the order and content of subsequent actions. A tool result can supply arguments for a later call, as in MCP-Bench and MCP-Universe [124, 125]. An interpretation of business context can determine which data to join or aggregate before a result is computed, as in DABstep [126]. A change of state can enable a previously unavailable operation: ToolSandbox’s messaging example requires cellular service to be enabled before the message can be sent [127]. TimeArena’s simulations add duration and resource constraints: agents must schedule around unfinished actions and occupied objects while advancing independent work [128]. Feedback from execution can require revising the sequence. Execution feedback guides SheetAgent’s workbook editing loop, while Spreadsheet-RL provides recalculated values during execution in Microsoft Excel [45, 129]. In DeepPresenter, rendered slides guide revisions; VIGA likewise executes candidate programs and inspects the rendered scenes before continuing scene edits in BlenderBench [58, 130]. The agent must retain these observations and changes so later actions use the current state. In many organizational settings, completing a task is only one step within a broader operational process. A finished financial forecast, for example, may need to pass through compliance verification, managerial assessment, and budget allocation before taking effect, where advancing the work depends on procedural rules and interactions with other participants. 20 3.4 Workflow Level 

The Workflow Level widens what is delegated from a single objective to the operational process around it. The process carries the objective through stages with distinct responsibilities and conditions for advancement, and each stage establishes an output, decision, or confirmation on which another stage or participant relies. The agent therefore coordinates the handoffs between stages and the procedural requirements that govern them, including cases where intermediate findings change the remaining work. Because these stages belong to the delegated work, completion covers the process outcome as well as the final output (Section 2.1): a correct forecast still cannot take effect while a required approval is missing. CXBench scores final outcomes and process compliance separately in regulated customer-service tasks, where a run that reaches the correct final state but fails a process check, such as providing a required disclosure or obtaining consent, counts as a false pass [131]. The same criterion separates workflow stages from the stages an agent system introduces into its own execution. Anthropic’s Research system, for example, runs a research stage, in which a lead agent decides how many search subagents to create and adds more when the evidence is insufficient, followed by a citation stage that attaches sources to the final report [132]. The requester, however, asks for a sourced answer to a research question, and even an explicit request for multi-agent execution specifies how the system should work without defining any stage on which the result depends. Any other arrangement of stages that yielded an equally well-sourced report would complete the request just as well, so the research and citation stages organize the execution of a multi-step objective rather than forming part of the delegated work. 

3.4.1 Carrying Work through Functional Stages 

Stages group actions around an output or decision that subsequent work requires. A handoff can make an artifact available under an agreed specification, confirm an input, or authorize further work. In WorkArena++, employee-onboarding tasks connect user creation with ordering and assigning hardware [133]. A similar handoff occurs in an OfficeBench training task: the agent reads an Excel participant list, creates calendar events, and emails the training details to participants [76]. The same dependence on stage outputs appears in artifact production. MetaGPT follows the standard operating procedures of a software team: a requirements document guides architecture and interface design, then implementation and testing [54]. SlideRL uses research findings and an outline to guide slide generation and revision [134]. EXP-Bench links experimental design and implementation to execution and conclusions, while PaperBench requires implementing and executing a paper’s experiments to reproduce its empirical results [135, 136]. DAComp makes the handoffs explicit in pipeline tasks that connect cleaning, entity integration, and aggregation under specified data contracts [137]. 

3.4.2 Coordinating Workflow Progress 

Once a stage has produced its output, assessment, confirmation, and policy conditions determine when work can advance. In CHI-Bench’s healthcare administrative tasks, assessment can yield a decision or a request for missing information [138]; τ -bench requires user confirmation before specified database updates [29]. In EnterpriseOps-Gym, warranty status determines whether the agent records a customer interaction and schedules a warranty discussion [139]. AutomationBench’s meeting-conflict task applies a spreadsheet priority policy, marks the lower-priority meeting for rescheduling, and reports the decision to Slack [140]. Progress also depends on other participants. In τ 2-bench’s telecommunications tasks, the agent coordinates its actions with changes made by a tool-using simulated user [30]. In EntCollabBench, roles with different tools and data access pass intermediate outputs to one another, while TheAgentCompany includes tasks requiring information from simulated coworkers [31, 38]. In Collaborative Gym, the agent’s literature search and the human’s feedback feed revisions to a shared related-work draft [141]. Retaining pending changes and their prerequisites lets the agent integrate each reply into the current workflow. These dependencies can extend across repeated operations: E-Commerce Bench connects procurement, fulfillment, returns, and cash-flow decisions over a simulated year [33]. 21 3.5 Workspace Level 

In the preceding forms, the request or task setup largely specifies which objects the work involves. As agents become able to work across entire code repositories and an organization’s documents, emails, and chats [11, 12, 142], a request can state only the goal. The agent must then establish which objects are relevant and how their relationships affect completion. A report may depend on a particular workbook version, while an approved decision may need to be reflected in several records. The Workspace Level makes discovering and following these relationships part of the delegated responsibility. The agent locates relevant objects, reconciles their evidence and versions, and, when the task requires updates, preserves consistency among the affected objects. 

3.5.1 Locating Relevant Objects in a Workspace 

Relevant material may be distributed across documents and spreadsheets, message histories, code repositories, or linked business records. Workspace-Bench makes identifying relevant objects and their dependencies part of the task [36]. GraphForge constructs assignments requiring comparison, reconciliation, or derivation across files, while WorkGenesis requires drawing on public source files and synthesized companion attachments to produce occupational deliverables [143, 144]. OfficeQA Pro’s full-corpus tasks require finding the pages needed for an answer, while DRBench’s application-based tasks require retrieving enterprise material for a research report [145, 146]. Finding the right page can itself require reading charts and layouts, as in ViDoRe, introduced with ColPali [147]. As a retrieved object points to further sources, the relevant context develops through these connections. 

3.5.2 Following Dependencies across Workspace Objects 

The connections uncovered during search determine how retrieved objects fit into the task. They may be explicit references, shared entities, or relationships of derivation and version. In CRMArena, identifying which order a customer refers to requires relating product information to that customer’s purchase history [148]. LOCA-bench makes these cross-source relationships explicit in a task adapted from Toolathlon: assembling an exam spreadsheet requires matching Canvas announcements and emails to the correct courses [149, 150]. In Loc-Bench, repository tasks similarly require locating the files and functions relevant to an issue description [151]. Repository repair extends this process to modifying code and testing the affected components [3, 16, 152, 153]. Spider 2.0’s DBT tasks extend this dependence to data-transformation projects: the agent reads SQL, YAML, and Markdown files to complete a missing transformation and check the resulting database state [154]. Using related objects together can also require reconciling what they say. Agora tasks require deriving a numerical answer from documents whose terminology, units, and time periods must be reconciled, while HERB queries connect people, products, and release events across heterogeneous enterprise records [155, 156]. APEX-Agents makes source selection concrete in a valuation task that combines the implied share price in a discounted cash-flow model with earnings per share from a financial statement for a specified date [157]. Tracing historical versions and derived-content relationships helps establish which sources support the conclusion [36]. Object relationships also determine what must be rechecked when work changes. In OSWorld 2.0, revised budget and vendor constraints arriving through TeamChat require the agent to reconsider affected entries in a purchase-order form [32]. Linking the updated constraints in the chat to the affected form entries identifies which earlier work needs revision. Discovering these dependencies exercises work openness; maintaining the result as relevant inputs change exercises persistence (Section 2.2). 

3.6 Synthesis: Work Scope and Capability Demands 

Across the five work forms, the foundational capabilities introduced in Section 2 support work over an expanding set of dependencies. As an assignment grows, an interpretation of the evidence can affect decisions and changes across several stages or objects. The agent must carry those dependencies through the work and use feedback from the resulting state to decide what can proceed and what needs revision. Work complexity thus scales through the web of dependencies that these capabilities must manage in concert. 22 Table 3 relates the five work forms to the capability requirements introduced in Section 2.3. Work at the Workspace Level requires tracking the sources and dependencies behind a result, with consistency checks extending to affected objects when updates are required. 

Table 3 Capability demands across the five Work Forms                                    

> Work Form Perception and Understanding Reasoning and De-cision Making Planning and Co-ordination Execution and State Manage-ment Verification, Re-covery, and Re-planning
> Conversation Level Interpret supplied ev-idence and context Derive supported an-swers and judgments Organize local rea-soning when needed Produce the re-quested response or specification Check evidence support, constraints, and output validity Atomic Action Level Identify the target object and local state Select an appropriate operation Establish a valid lo-cal action path Commit the intended local change Confirm the target ef-fect Multi-step Level Track intermediate evidence and state Adapt decisions to in-termediate results Decompose a coher-ent objective Maintain state across related actions Check intermediate and final outcomes Workflow Level Identify stages, rules, and shared state Reason about prereq-uisites and policies Coordinate stages, branches, and handoffs Commit consequen-tial state transitions Validate stage tran-sitions and workflow outcomes Workspace Level Discover relevant ob-jects, relations, ver-sions, and authority Reconcile evidence across objects Plan over object and dependency structure Track object state; preserve consistency when updating re-lated objects Check source lineage, freshness, and depen-dencies

This dependency-centered view also clarifies where a work agent needs improvement: missing evidence can interrupt a sequence of actions, an incorrect decision can propagate into later stages, and a changed constraint can invalidate dependent outputs. Section 4 examines how experience from such work can improve the model, the harness, and their interaction, while Section 5 considers how to assess the resulting work and the progress made by these improvements. 

## 4 Optimization: From Work Experience to System Capability 

The capabilities required by work agents must be cultivated via systematic optimization. As discussed in Section 2, this optimization can take place at both the model and harness levels. Model updates improve interpretation, reasoning and action selection, while harness updates enhance the interfaces, tools, memory and execution workflows that support the model. These improvements draw on diverse corpora and experience gathered during work, including observations of the environment, action trajectories and feedback on task outcomes. As illustrated in Figure 5, Section 4.1 examines the environments, data, and feedback that provide learning signals. Section 4.2 examines how pre-training, mid-training, and post-training develop model capabilities, while Section 4.3 explores improvements to interfaces, workflows, and memory. Finally, Section 4.4 examines how models and harnesses can adapt together and support continued self-improvement. 

4.1 Training Environments, Data, and Rewards 

Agents learn to perform work through environments, data and feedback. Environments provide tasks for interaction, while corpora offer relevant knowledge and trajectories show how actions affect task progress. Feedback helps agents identify successful decisions and outcomes. For example, when preparing a report from multiple workbooks, an agent needs to understand how source values relate to calculations and reported findings, and how to update affected results when the source data changes.
