4.1.1 Work Environment Construction 

Training environments convert work requirements into tasks that agents can execute and evaluate. Each task includes an initial workspace, available actions, a request, and checks for evaluating the results. These components can be constructed from existing work records. For example, daVinci-Env reconstructs software tasks from repositories, and Spreadsheet-RL uses paired workbooks and an Excel runtime [129, 158]. Real-world tasks often depend on information spread across documents, applications, and people. Therefore, training environments should preserve these dependencies so that agents can learn which actions to take and how to verify their results. 

Grounding tasks in work materials. Recent pipelines connect work materials to requirements in several ways. NexForge organizes task generation around capabilities and task forms, while WorkGenesis builds occupational requests with supporting resources [144, 159]. GraphForge represents evidence across retrieved files as a graph, then uses it to generate instructions and source-linked rubrics [143]. WorkForge starts from expert workflow plans, retrieves real files, and identifies facts that guide the instructions, solution plans, and programmatic and semantic checks [160]. WorkBorne organizes tasks around artifact transitions: a specified 25 actor transforms input materials toward reference outputs [161]. Together, these approaches cover different aspects of task construction, from generating realistic requirements to grounding them in evidence and defining the expected results. 

Constructing shared and changing state. Tasks spanning multiple applications also require consistent states and operations. Agent World Model creates database-backed tool environments, EnvCraft builds sequences of dependent tasks, and AgentMercury constructs business environments with shared entities and constraints [162–164]. CoworkEnv jointly generates requests, service records, completion checks, and permitted actions, then validates their consistency before collecting teacher trajectories [166]. Cowork-Gym introduces a stateful user who can provide information, revise requirements, and modify the shared workspace [167]. Such changes can affect later actions. For example, an updated customer record may change which operation is valid, while a revised workbook may invalidate an earlier analysis. WorkRover repeatedly checks and repairs consistency among the context, task, harness, and verifier [168]. These methods highlight the importance of maintaining a valid and consistent environment as tasks progress. CompoWorld extends environment construction to tasks involving multiple services [165]. It builds reusable services with typed states and common interfaces, uses a world model for tools that are difficult to implement reliably, and samples dependency graphs across services. Each service maintains its own state, while the agent transfers information between services through observations and actions. This allows cross-service dependencies to be controlled alongside the number of services. In a matched comparison using the same backbone, training procedure, and 1K SFT trajectories, AutomationBench success increases from 12.83% with single-service environments to 33.33% with composed environments. 

Scaling coverage, depth, and validation. Training environments can scale by adding more workspaces, more tasks within each workspace, or more rollouts per task. These choices expose agents to different materials, task dependencies, and ways to complete or recover from work. WorkBorne compares broader artifact coverage with more transitions per corpus under matched trajectory budgets [161]. Broader coverage performs better at 1K, 3K, and 5K trajectories, while both strategies achieve similar results at 8K. WorkForge reports that increasing training data from 1K to 16K trajectories improves ALE by 13.0 points and WildClaw by 8.4 points [160]. Its comparisons using equal numbers of trajectories also favor longer interactions, although these trajectories differ in task content and decision depth. Together, these findings highlight the importance of tracking both task diversity and interaction structure alongside training volume. Reported dataset sizes also refer to different outputs of generation pipelines. WorkForge constructs 16,665 tasks, GraphForge retains 2,169 training trajectories from 2,153 selected tasks, and WorkBorne produces 7,882 task environments and 15,485 trajectories from 1,061 artifact corpora [143, 160, 161]. These counts are difficult to compare without specifying what is measured and at which stage. Thus, we suggest reporting generation attempts, accepted tasks, usable trajectories, and verification costs to assess how efficiently environment construction produces useful training experience. Validation is equally important as environments scale. An executable task may still contain incomplete requirements, inconsistent sources, or a verifier that accepts incorrect results. Validation must establish both that a task can be completed and that its verifier accepts correct solutions while detecting errors. It must also cover any new materials introduced during scaling. In WorkForge’s matched 1K-trajectory ablation, combining programmatic and semantic checks achieves an ALE score of 22.11, compared with 15.62 for semantic-only and 14.89 for programmatic-only selection [160]. GraphForge’s corruption audit shows that deleting a worksheet reduces the target rubric score by 0.377, while finer row-level, numerical, and citation errors change scores by less than 0.03 on a 0–1 scale [143]. These results suggest combining source-based judgments with exact checks of critical values and using deliberate errors to test verifier reliability. Failed checks and their dependencies can also guide environment repairs, as discussed in Section 4.4.3. Beyond scale and validation, training environments vary in difficulty, openness, persistence, and business fidelity. Difficulty reflects the decisions and dependencies involved in completing a task, while openness reflects what the agent must discover during execution. Persistence concerns how long the task state and responsibilities remain relevant, and business fidelity describes how closely the materials and constraints reflect real work. Human participants may also provide clarification, corrections, approvals, or final assessments 26 at different stages. A useful training set should combine controlled tasks that expose specific failures with interactive workspaces that require multiple capabilities. Evaluation on new business tasks can then test whether these capabilities transfer to unfamiliar materials and conditions. 

4.1.2 Corpus and Trajectory Curation 

Corpora provide the materials agents work with, while trajectories record how agents use those materials to complete tasks. Documents, code, and completed artifacts offer domain knowledge and examples, while interaction records capture observations, actions, and their results. Curation selects and organizes these sources according to the capabilities being trained. For example, repository-level data can link issues to relevant files and edits, while execution trajectories also capture tool outputs and failed attempts leading to those changes [169]. Preserving this context helps models learn what actions to take and why. Task, workspace, and trajectory identifiers support coverage analysis and data splits based on shared sources. Interactive tasks also require records of changing requirements and actions taken by other participants [167]. CoworkEnv collects teacher demonstrations from validated environments, where SFT improves the mean Claw-Eval score from 46.6 to 64.1. The same environments also support student RL comparisons [166]. Generated trajectories should preserve the dependencies between actions. AgentTrek uses tutorials and guided replay to construct web interaction trajectories [170]. EnterpriseLab derives tool dependencies from enterprise schemas and reuses identifiers returned by earlier calls in later operations [171]. TDScaling organizes tools into business scenarios and generates examples for underrepresented domains and reasoning patterns [176]. Exploration offers another way to collect experience. SEE builds a verified graph of mobile interface transitions and generates tasks involving interruptions and recovery [177]. Scenario-based generation provides workflows with clear goals, while exploration captures states that standard demonstrations may miss. Successful trajectories show how to complete tasks, and recovery segments demonstrate how to proceed when execution is disrupted. Data selection should reflect the capabilities being trained. Artifact checks can filter for successful outcomes, while failed attempts may still provide useful examples for recovery and state tracking. OpenThoughts-Agent evaluates task sources, teachers, data mixtures, and filtering strategies through downstream training, including comparisons under matched token budgets [172]. CurateEvo uses development-set failures to improve data augmentation, filtering, and refinement [173]. As policies improve, repeated easy successes provide less useful information, while errors involving changed inputs, missed requirements, or costly recovery can guide further data collection. Construction and development data should remain separate from held-out evaluation tasks. When tasks share files, splitting data by workspace or source family provides a stronger test of whether models can generalize to unfamiliar materials and requirements. 

4.1.3 Feedback Design 

Feedback turns work requirements into learning signals. When success can be measured from the resulting workspace, direct checks are possible. Spreadsheet-RL recalculates edited workbooks in Excel and compares relevant regions against target workbooks [129]. Anchor derives business tasks and verifiers from constraint programs to check feasibility, solution quality, and relationships among records [174]. Other tasks require judgments about content and presentation. Preference-based reward learning uses human comparisons [178], GraphForge links rubric items to source evidence, and SlideRL evaluates slide structure, rendering, and content [134, 143]. CogEvol also checks rendered content and browser interactions [179]. The appropriate feedback depends on the output, which may require checks of its values, organization, and behavior. Require-ments specify what counts as success, while verifiers assess the supporting evidence. Keeping these roles separate allows missing checks or parsing errors to be corrected without changing the task itself. Feedback can also focus on requirements that agents frequently miss. The Completion-Focused Rubric Reward of CompoWorld assigns greater weight to criteria with lower pass rates within a rollout group [165]. It rewards partial progress but gives the maximum reward only when all criteria are satisfied. This encourages agents to address the remaining steps needed to complete a workflow. Final outcomes alone do not reveal which actions contributed to success. Intermediate feedback helps connect 27 results to earlier decisions [180, 181]. VERA restores workspace states and checks intermediate requirements using executable tests and rubrics [175]. WorkRover’s Verifier-to-Turn Credit Assignment uses recorded dependencies to assign bounded positive credit to earlier turns that contributed to verified progress [168]. For example, reading a source may receive credit if its information supports a later correct change. Stage checks identify when progress occurs, while dependency records help determine which earlier decisions contributed to it. Both require sufficient records of workspace states and their relationships. Reliable feedback must also account for changing requirements. Cowork-Gym links requirements to success conditions, supporting evidence, and checkers while allowing users to introduce new constraints [167]. Main-taining current requirements alongside relevant artifacts helps agents detect omissions when earlier instructions leave the active context or source data changes. Verification also has a cost. Structural and state checks can detect common errors efficiently, while semantic inspection and human assessment handle requirements that need more context. As execution becomes faster and more parallel, verification may become a bottleneck. Thus, we propose using dependencies among sources and artifacts to determine which checks need to be repeated, which evidence remains valid, and when further assessment is necessary. Feedback design should also account for the verification and rework required before an output can be accepted. 

4.2 Model Optimization 

Model optimization develops the knowledge and decision-making capabilities agents need to perform work. Pre-training builds broad semantic and computational knowledge; mid-training adapts this foundation to domain materials, interaction patterns, and long context; post-training strengthens behavior through demonstrations, execution feedback, and teacher guidance. Figure 6 connects these roles. The model first learns about work objects, then how to use their interaction histories, and how to improve decisions from task outcomes. The stages differ in their data mixtures, prediction targets, and training schedules, while token prediction and agent trajectories contribute across stages. 

4.2.1 Pre-training 

Pre-training establishes the world knowledge and general capabilities that work agents rely on to understand unfamiliar tasks and environments. Through large-scale learning from text, code, mathematics, and other materials, models acquire factual knowledge, domain concepts, common conventions, and computational skills. These are essential for professional work, where requests often assume knowledge that users do not explicitly provide. For example, an agent revising a budget report must understand financial categories and units, interpret relationships between tables and written claims, and recognize how formulas or code produce numerical results. This background knowledge allows agents to interpret workspace materials, understand task requirements, and reason about possible actions before adapting to specific workflows. Work materials can in turn enrich pre-training with structured and multimodal prediction signals. For autoregressive language models, pre-training uses the standard next-token prediction (NTP) objective. Given a token sequence x, the model learns to predict each token from its preceding context by minimizing 

LNTP (θ) = −Ex∼D |x|

X

> t=1

log pθ (xt | x<t ), (3) where D denotes the pre-training corpus. Under this objective, the selection and organization of training data determine what knowledge and relationships the model encounters. DeepSeek-V4, for example, combines web data with mathematics, programming, and long technical documents [182]. Work materials further expose cell addresses, formulas, layout, and relations between inputs and computed outputs. DocLLM uses text-infilling pre-training over document text and bounding-box layout [183]. Such structure can support executable prediction targets: TAPEX pre-trains a model to predict SQL execution results from queries and serialized tables, improving downstream table reasoning [184]. This illustrates how structured data can develop computational skills alongside factual knowledge. Additionally, work agents must recognize relevant knowledge when it appears in different forms. A task requirement may be described briefly in a user request, explained in detail in a reference document, or 28 Model Optimization

Figure 6 Training emphases that develop the model. Illustrative samples distinguish visible context from prediction targets and feedback. Pre-training builds semantic, computational, structural, and visual knowledge. Mid-training enriches domain knowledge, introduces interaction patterns, and develops long-context use in preparation for subsequent optimization. Post-training uses demonstrations, execution rewards, teacher guidance, and accumulated feedback to supervise decisions, trajectories, artifacts, or earlier attempts. Links to the model denote learning signals, not live execution. Inputs and objectives can overlap across stages; the samples do not prescribe a shared dataset or universal training recipe. Supervision granularity differs across the four post-training groups. 

reflected in the content of an output artifact. Kimi K2 addresses this challenge by rewriting knowledge-rich passages in different styles and from different perspectives while preserving their original meaning [185]. Its early-checkpoint experiment reports 28.94% SimpleQA accuracy for ten distinct rephrasings used once, versus 23.76% for ten repetitions of the original text. For work agents, this principle could extend to aligned views of the same content, such as source cells, rendered charts, and report passages. Preserving their correspondences could provide training signals for maintaining semantic consistency across formats and associating claims with supporting data. Working environments also contain visual information that agents must interpret alongside text and code. Kimi K2.5 jointly trains on text and visual inputs, including screenshots and image–code pairs; its pre-training corpus also includes dense OCR, multi-page documents, GUI screenshots, and fine-grained grounding annotations [186]. Dense work artifacts require reading small text, distinguishing visually similar elements, and interpreting spatial relationships. Pairing rendered workbooks or slides with native object structure could extend this supervision to locating cells, interpreting chart elements, and connecting visible content to its underlying representation. Such data could strengthen transferable perception and understanding capabilities (Figure 3), establishing the knowledge foundation for subsequent reasoning, planning, execution, and verification. 

4.2.2 Mid-training 

Targeted Knowledge Enrichment. Work agents often encounter tasks that require knowledge beyond the information provided in a user request. Understanding an unfamiliar repository, interpreting business records, or following domain-specific conventions may depend on knowledge that is underrepresented in general 29 pre-training data. Mid-training addresses this gap by increasing the density of specialized and long-tail knowledge while preserving the relationships among relevant materials. GLM-5 organizes related files, issues, pull requests, and commits into training sequences, then increases the proportion of long documents and synthetic agent trajectories in later stages [187]. Step 3.5 Flash begins with software engineering and tool-use data at 32K context, retaining 21% pre-training replay, before expanding to 128K with long documents, synthetic reasoning, and agent data [188]. Kimi K2.5 similarly combines high-quality text and multimodal materials while extending context from 32K to 256K [186]. These approaches increase exposure to specialized work knowledge while using replay to preserve broader capabilities. For work agents, organizing knowledge around related files, requirements, and revisions is particularly useful, as understanding a work object often requires information distributed across multiple sources. 

Agent Interaction Familiarization. Domain knowledge alone cannot prepare an agent for the interactions required to complete work. An agent must also understand what a tool call changes, how to interpret its response, and what to do when an operation fails or produces an unexpected result. Mid-training can introduce these interaction patterns before post-training begins to optimize task behavior. DeepSeek-V4 incorporates agentic coding data at this stage [182]. Nemotron combines repositories, pull requests, edit histories, and build logs in a 500B-token mixture at 256K context, including 10% agent traces and 20% pre-training replay [189]. With subsequent SFT held fixed, this stage improves SWE-bench Verified Pass@1 from 60.7% to 64.5%. daVinci-Dev further combines trajectories reconstructed from pull requests with trajectories collected through actual tool execution [169]. Its mid-training uses both successful and failed executions, while later SFT focuses on test-passing trajectories. This distinction is important for work agents, as unsuccessful interactions can expose errors and intermediate states that success-only demonstrations may omit. Learning from these records helps models become familiar with the observation, action, and state-change patterns involved in execution. This provides a stronger starting point for post-training, where feedback can focus on improving decisions and completing tasks reliably. 

Long-Context Learning and Post-Training Readiness. Work agents frequently perform tasks in which later decisions depend on information or changes from much earlier interactions. For example, modifying a source workbook may affect several calculations and invalidate parts of a previously generated report. Simply extending the context window does not ensure that a model can identify these dependencies or reconstruct the current workspace state. TrajLong addresses this problem by transforming interaction histories into retrieval, information-combination, and file-state reconstruction tasks using full-token next-token prediction [190]. Under a fixed 40B-token budget and identical later SFT, these tasks outperform raw-trajectory training on all seven reported ablation benchmarks. Function-aware fill-in-the-middle training similarly uses code dependencies to select functions or function groups for reconstruction, improving the mean across SWE-bench Verified and Lite from 13.95 to 15.60 in a controlled 7B comparison [191]. The choice of prediction targets also matters. In Nemotron’s 100B-token ablation, masking tool responses while retaining them as context raises SWE-bench Verified Pass@1 from 27.5% to 37.8% after identical SFT [189]. This distinction is relevant to agent training because tool responses provide evidence for subsequent decisions, even when the model does not need to generate those responses itself. Efficient access to long histories presents another challenge. Qwen3.8-Flash-Next introduces sparse attention during continued training at 256K context to reduce the cost of accessing relevant information [192], connecting this stage to the memory mechanisms in Section 4.3.3. Ultimately, the value of mid-training should be measured by its contribution to later optimization. Step 3.5 Flash reports stronger and more stable RL gains after incorporating task knowledge and tool-use patterns [188], while Nemotron compares recipes through identical SFT runs because intermediate model metrics do not reliably predict downstream agent performance [189]. 

4.2.3 Post-training 

Post-training develops the policies that allow work agents to apply their knowledge to real tasks. Beyond understanding a request, an agent must select appropriate actions, respond to changes in the workspace, and determine whether its results satisfy the requirements. Different training methods provide supervision for these decisions. Demonstrations show how tasks can be completed, execution rewards evaluate the consequences of actions, teacher guidance improves decisions at states encountered by the agent, and retrospective learning extracts useful information from previous attempts. Table 5 summarizes these methods according to their 30 learning signals and update mechanisms. 

Learning from demonstrations. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) teaches agents to follow instructions, use tools, and produce completed work through examples [193–195]. Synthetic instructions provide additional supervision before task-specific interaction [196]. For work agents, demonstrations can capture both the steps taken during execution and the structure of an accepted deliverable. Kimi K2 generates tool-use trajectories by constructing domains, tools, and tasks, simulating interactions, and filtering the results, while also incorporating real execution environments into post-training [185]. Interactive demonstrations teach agents how to respond to observations and tool feedback [197, 198]. Artifact-based supervision instead connects task requirements to completed outputs. CogEvol trains on verified representations of slides and interactive pages [179], while GraphForge uses source-linked rubrics to select generated solutions for rejection fine-tuning [143]. CoworkEnv provides curated teacher executions that improve multi-turn performance on Claw-Eval, with the same executable environments supporting subsequent RL [166]. Together, these approaches teach agents how to carry out tasks and what acceptable results should look like. 

Learning through execution rewards. Reinforcement learning improves an agent’s policy through the outcomes of its own interactions [199–201]. Web navigation, device control, and repository repair provide different environments in which actions can be executed and evaluated [202–204]. For artifact-producing tasks, Spreadsheet-RL evaluates the resulting workbook, while SlideRL combines structural, visual, and content feedback to assess generated slides [129, 134]. However, work agents often complete tasks through many dependent steps, making it difficult to determine which earlier decisions contributed to the final result. Intermediate rewards and credit assignment help address this problem. ADMIRE derives adaptive milestones from successful GUI trajectories, while HiPER learns hierarchical policies with subgoals and associated value estimates [205, 206]. GiGPO combines episode-level advantages with comparisons of actions taken from repeated environment states [207]. WorkRover uses recorded dependencies to assign credit to earlier turns that support verified progress [168]. These methods provide different ways to connect final outcomes with intermediate decisions, allowing agents to learn from both completed work and progress made along the way. Reliable execution feedback also requires accurate records of the interactions being trained on. Mercor and SkyRL’s knowledge-work recipe uses 1,928 expert-created tasks and emphasizes environment repair, exact token histories, and small-set overfitting tests before scaling RL [208]. Its loss ablations compare weighting at the token, sequence, and prompt-group levels, with prompt-group weighting reducing the influence of unusually long trajectories. The resulting 397B model improves held-out APEX-Agents Pass@1 from 16.11% to 27.29%. These practices are particularly important for work agents, where tool failures, inconsistent environments, and long execution histories can make rewards unreliable. Preserving the exact observations and actions behind each reward helps distinguish policy errors from infrastructure failures and keeps learning aligned with the interaction that actually occurred. 

Learning from teacher guidance. Demonstrations alone may not cover the states an agent encounters during its own execution, especially after incorrect actions or unexpected tool responses. On-policy distillation addresses this gap by providing teacher guidance at states reached by the student [209]. TCOD gradually increases student-controlled interaction, while Guided-OPD reduces teacher-controlled turns and combines teacher imitation with distillation on student-generated states [210, 211]. This allows supervision to follow the developing policy more closely [212, 213]. Step-wise on-policy distillation adjusts guidance based on student–teacher divergence [214]. Other methods improve supervision at specific decisions. LiteGUI selects a human-verified valid action that matches the student’s attempted action, while Gated Hindsight Distillation uses a later screenshot to guide an earlier decision [215, 216]. Foundation-model training also uses on-policy distillation to combine specialized capabilities or preserve them across training stages [182, 187]. For work agents, these methods are useful because guidance can address errors and partial progress encountered during actual execution, rather than relying only on previously collected demonstrations. 

Learning from accumulated feedback. Work agents can also learn from information accumulated across attempts, including corrections, execution feedback, and completed results [217–219]. SDPO provides textual feedback to a self-teacher and distills its improved predictions into the acting policy, while Skill-SD additionally conditions the teacher on short summaries of earlier attempts [220–222]. RePro uses completed attempts to teach models which intermediate decisions indicate useful progress [223]. These approaches reuse information 31 revealed during execution to improve future behavior [224]. For example, when generating a report from multiple sources, demonstrations can teach the initial workflow, execution rewards can assess the completed report, and teacher guidance can help correct errors encountered during revision. Retrospective feedback can then turn these corrections into useful experience for subsequent tasks. Section 4.4.3 extends this process beyond model updates to systems that also improve their environments, external procedures, and methods of learning.

32 4.3 Harness Optimization 

Work agents rely on external support to inspect information, execute operations, and manage work across multiple steps. Even with a capable model, missing evidence, unreliable tools, or poorly organized procedures can prevent successful task completion. Harness optimization improves this external support through observation and action interfaces, execution workflows, and reusable experience. Interfaces determine what the model can observe and how it interacts with the workspace. Workflows organize execution, verification, recovery, and delivery, while context management, memory, and skills preserve task progress and useful procedures across interactions. Execution traces reveal where these components need improvement, such as missing information, repeated tool errors, or unnecessary rework. Table 6 compares representative methods based on what they change and how feedback guides their optimization.

33 4.3.1 Observation and Action Interfaces 

Observation interfaces determine what information an agent can inspect before making a decision. For work agents, this often involves locating relevant evidence within large repositories, documents, or tool outputs. SWE-agent introduces agent-oriented repository operations that make software files easier to search, inspect, and edit [3]. TACO further improves observations by discovering terminal-output filters, refining them during execution, and reusing effective rules across tasks [225]. These filters reduce unnecessary output while preserving relevant state and error information. The main consideration is whether an interface provides the evidence needed for the next decision without consuming excessive context or requiring repeated inspection. Action interfaces determine how an agent translates its decisions into operations on files, applications, and services. Unclear tool descriptions or inconsistent argument conventions can cause repeated errors, even when the intended operation is appropriate. Trace-Free+ trains a tool-description generator, gradually moving from supervision based on execution traces to generation using tool descriptions alone [226]. JTPRO jointly optimizes global instructions and tool descriptions using execution feedback [227]. The former learns tool-use conventions that can transfer across tool sets, while the latter coordinates instructions within a particular environment. Both aim to improve tool selection and argument generation, helping agents carry out the operations required by a task. Executable support can also correct errors during tool execution. PRISM analyzes tool traces, state changes, and errors to improve prompts, middleware, or both [228]. Middleware can correct arguments, check prerequisites, and provide error information that supports recovery. These mechanisms are particularly important when work agents modify shared records or related objects. For example, checking a file version before writing helps ensure that an edit applies to the same object the agent previously inspected. Tool responses should also indicate what changed so the agent can decide whether to continue or recover. Evaluating interfaces with the same model and tasks helps isolate improvements from external support. In the Mercor/SkyRL study, correcting tool behavior and observation handling improves the base agent before weight training, while the training gains also transfer to a held-out code-based harness [208]. Such comparisons help distinguish improvements in the execution environment from improvements learned by the model. 

4.3.2 Workflows and Execution Control 

Workflow optimization improves how an agent organizes and executes work across multiple steps [241, 242]. The editable components range from prompts [243–245] and connections between model calls [246, 247] to executable harness code [248, 249]. Prompt optimization can use feedback-guided revision [250, 251], reinforcement learning [252], or evolutionary search [253, 254]. More recent approaches extend these ideas to complete execution procedures. GEPA uses execution traces and outcomes to refine prompts [229], AFlow searches over workflow graphs [230], and Meta-Harness modifies harness code [231]. These methods provide different levels of control over how an agent performs work [255]. For example, an improved workflow may inspect additional evidence before editing, verify intermediate results before dependent operations, or introduce a recovery step after failure. Its effectiveness should be measured by whether these changes improve execution on subsequent tasks. Artifact production illustrates how feedback from individual tasks can improve reusable workflows. AutoDesign uses rendering, validation, and critic feedback to revise a poster, then analyzes recurring failures to propose changes to its design harness [232]. This separates improving the current deliverable from improving the procedure used for future tasks. JIT-Agent takes a related approach by training a separate model to construct task-specific harnesses using execution reward, latency, and cost from a frozen executor [233]. The designer learns how to organize execution and produces an explicit procedure for the acting agent. These approaches show how experience from completed work can guide the design of more effective workflows without requiring changes to the underlying executor. Execution control must also preserve progress when work is interrupted, requirements change, or responsibilities are transferred. For ongoing tasks, a workflow needs clear conditions for continuing, revising previous results, and determining when delivery is complete. These conditions relate to the requirements and responsibilities discussed in Section 2. For example, a report awaiting source confirmation should retain that dependency, while 34 a completed deliverable should be checked against the latest requirements before the task closes. Repeated inspections, retries, and human corrections provide evidence of where workflow changes could reduce delays and rework. Methods based on retrospective self-preference, adaptive curricula, and task-dependent harness routing further use feedback to select and deploy suitable procedures [256–258]. Evaluating both the quality of accepted results and the effort required to produce them helps ensure that workflow optimization improves real task execution. 

4.3.3 Long Context, Memory and Skills 

Context management determines which parts of a growing work history remain available for each decision. Work agents may accumulate lengthy tool outputs, repeated observations, and information that becomes outdated as the workspace changes. A larger context window alone does not resolve these issues. MemGPT manages information between active context and external storage, while TACO adjusts the amount and form of tool output retained during execution [225, 234]. For ongoing work, the active context must also preserve current requirements, relevant object versions, and unresolved dependencies. These records help agents determine whether earlier observations and decisions remain valid. Effective context management therefore supports efficient access to information and reliable continuation after interruptions or revisions. Memory optimization focuses on preserving useful experience across tasks. ReasoningBank extracts strategies from successful and failed attempts for later retrieval [235], while ACE maintains an evolving playbook through generation, reflection, and curation [236]. Skills extend this idea by representing reusable procedures that guide future execution. SkillOpt improves procedural documents through validated edits [237], SkillAlpha learns editing policies and evaluates them through downstream execution [238], and CodeSkill extracts, revises, and maintains a library of coding skills [239]. These approaches allow agents to reuse lessons from previous work rather than rediscovering procedures for every task. Execution feedback also helps determine which instructions remain useful and which should be revised as new situations arise. As reusable experience accumulates, agents must decide which procedures to retain, how to combine them, and when to apply them. SkillSmith addresses interactions and dependencies among skills under a limited context budget and considers which operations should be represented as skills or executable tools [240]. Stable operations can be implemented as tools, while skills provide guidance on when and how to use them. This distinction is important for work agents that repeatedly perform related tasks with different materials and constraints. Task-specific state and reusable experience also require different update schedules. A change to a source workbook should immediately update the current task record, while a revised reporting procedure should be validated across multiple tasks before broader reuse. Recording the sources and dependencies of both enables targeted updates. For example, a reporting skill may describe how to reconcile information from several workbooks, while the task record identifies which workbook version has been checked and which approval remains pending. Maintaining this distinction allows agents to reuse established procedures while keeping their execution aligned with the current workspace. 

4.4 Model--Harness Coupling and Joint Optimization 

The effectiveness of a work agent depends on how well its model and harness work together. The model must learn to use the information and tools provided by the harness, while the harness must offer interfaces and procedures that the model can use reliably. This raises an important question of where improvements should be implemented. A capability may be learned through model training, provided through retrieved context, encoded as a reusable skill, or implemented directly in control code. We examine how models are trained within a harness, how the two components can be optimized together, and how repeated execution can improve both the system and its optimization process. 4.4.1 Harness-Aware Policy Training 

A training harness determines what a policy can observe and do. Tool schemas define available operations and their arguments, context management controls access to previous interactions, and execution rules determine how actions affect the workspace. Harness-aware training allows models to learn under these conditions. Spreadsheet-RL trains agents with a spreadsheet runtime and toolset, while UI-Copilot provides structured observations, progress summaries, and a copilot for retrieval and calculation [129, 259]. Through these interactions, the policy learns when to rely on internal reasoning and when to use external tools. The deployment harness must therefore provide the interfaces and support that the policy has learned to use. Training systems can also optimize a policy while preserving the agent’s existing execution process. Agent Lightning connects policy optimization with agent execution, allowing the harness to manage interactions while the training system learns from the resulting decision records [260]. The available tools, memory access, and execution rules all influence the experience collected during training. For example, a model trained with compressed observations needs to learn how to retrieve information that is no longer available in its active context. The training harness therefore shapes the situations and decisions the policy learns to handle, even when only model weights are updated. Models can also learn to construct tools for future tasks. SMITH alternates between tool construction and tool use, adds generated tools to a reusable pool, and rewards their validity and downstream usefulness [271]. As the available tools evolve, the policy learns how to use the capabilities it creates. For work agents, this provides a way to turn repeated operations into reusable executable tools while learning when to construct and invoke them [272]. Feedback from subsequent execution helps determine which generated tools are useful, allowing the model to improve based on their contributions to later tasks. 

4.4.2 Joint Model--Harness Adaptation 

Joint adaptation coordinates model learning with changes to external support, which can be revised at a different pace [273]. BetterTogether alternates between prompt and weight optimization, while Co-Harness uses improved harnesses to generate model-training trajectories and then adapts the harness to the updated model [261, 263]. Fast-Slow Training similarly alternates prompt search with blocks of reinforcement-learning updates [262]. In these approaches, each component is optimized using experience collected with the current version of the other. This interaction matters because a procedure that compensates for an earlier model’s limitations may become unnecessary after training, while a stronger model may be able to use workflows that were previously unreliable. 37 Memory and skills provide concrete components for joint adaptation. UI-Mem extracts experience during GUI reinforcement learning and updates memory guidance as the policy improves, while SkillRL maintains a skill library alongside policy training [264, 265]. VERA alternates rubric-guided model updates with validated additions, revisions, and removals in a skill library organized around work stages [175]. SIA uses execution trajectories and reward changes to decide between scaffold editing and policy reinforcement [267]. These approaches allow improvements to target the model, its external support, or both, depending on the observed failures and the capabilities already available. An important question is where learned experience should be stored. Component Routing compares experience incorporated through weight updates with the same experience provided as context [274]. In its evaluated GUI settings, recurring patterns for locating interface elements favor weight updates, while state-dependent procedures and facts benefit more from context. The effectiveness of transferred procedures also depends on the receiving model. For work agents, this suggests learning recurring behavioral patterns in model weights while keeping changing facts, task commitments, and operating rules in external records. To evaluate these choices, we propose comparing (M0, H 0), (M1, H 0), (M0, H 1), and (M1, H 1) under consistent task conditions and compatible interfaces. For ongoing work, each configuration can resume from the same saved workspace after an identical source change or user intervention. Comparing these runs with fresh starts would reveal whether updates preserve unfinished dependencies, support necessary rechecks, and improve task completion while accounting for verification costs and human intervention. 

4.4.3 Self-Improvement and Recursive Self-Improvement 

Work agents may operate over extended periods while the environments in which they work continue to change. New tools, data formats, business rules, and organizational processes can gradually make previously reliable procedures less effective. Although context and memory can help agents recognize these changes, substantial shifts may require persistent updates to their models, workflows, skills, and verification mechanisms. Self-improvement addresses this need by using execution experience to adapt the agent and its supporting environment. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) goes further by improving the mechanisms through which the system identifies limitations, develops new capabilities, and evaluates its own progress. These two directions offer complementary benefits for work agents. Self-improvement supports continued adaptation to changing work, while RSI offers a potential path toward more fundamental growth in agent capabilities. 

Adapting the source of practice. Self-improvement allows agents to accumulate experience and revise the capabilities and procedures they use in later work. Socratic-SWE extracts strategies and recurring failures into a skill registry, then alternates task generation and solving through a shared policy with role-specific objectives [269]. Generated tasks guide subsequent training, while new execution traces inform later task generation. CurateEvo uses development failures to improve data augmentation and filtering [173], while SkillRL and SLIM update a procedural library alongside policy training [265, 266]. These methods improve future behavior by changing what the agent practices and which procedures it can reuse. Beyond benchmark tasks, DMRL learns a policy that edits the skill instructions of a frozen advertising-system agent using online outcomes and estimates of longer-term effects [268]. Such feedback connects self-improvement to operational needs, where an agent must adjust its behavior as requirements and working conditions evolve. 

Maintaining reliable training environments. Continued learning also requires environments that provide valid tasks and feedback. GraphForge revises generated tasks and rubrics using execution feedback before collecting training trajectories [143], while WorkForge constructs and filters environments through a forward generation pipeline [160]. WorkRover supports repeated environment repair by diagnosing compatibility failures and testing candidate changes through Factorial Compatibility Replay [168]. It selects the smallest repair set that satisfies fixed requirements and preserves necessary dependencies, then validates the result through fresh execution. Its natural-defect study reports 70.3% safe repairs, compared with 60.7% for full regeneration, with rates remaining within 70.3–72.4% across three repair generations. Table 7 further compares regressions and token costs. These methods highlight the need to distinguish failures caused by the policy from defects in the training environment. An incorrect decision may require further learning, while missing materials or inconsistent verifiers require environment repair. Maintaining reliable learning environments helps agents improve from experience as their training needs change. 38 From adaptive improvement to RSI. Self-improvement can also be guided directly by the outcomes of real work. DMRL learns a policy that edits the skill instructions of a frozen advertising-system agent using online outcomes and estimates of longer-term effects [268]. This illustrates how business feedback can guide improvements to reusable procedures. RSI introduces a further possibility by allowing the optimization process itself to evolve. VERSE explores this direction by keeping the executor and optimizer model weights fixed while modifying the executor harness and the optimizer’s prompts, skills, tools, hooks, and notes [270]. Its controlled comparison evaluates execution verification and optimizer self-evolution separately and together, with the combined setting achieving the best results. Without executable checks, self-evolution selects the initial harness and produces a lower test result. Although these results are based on one evolution run per condition, they illustrate how changes to the optimization process can influence subsequent improvements. These studies provide different perspectives on how improvement should be evaluated. DMRL connects procedural updates to business outcomes [268], WorkRover examines the reliability and cost of repeated environment repairs [168], and VERSE separates the effects of optimizer evolution and executable verifica-tion [270]. For work agents, we propose extending these comparisons to longer periods and changing work conditions. Self-improvement can be evaluated by how effectively an agent adapts while preserving unfinished responsibilities and maintaining the quality of accepted work. RSI requires an additional comparison between the updated optimizer and its frozen predecessor, testing whether improvements to the optimization process enable more effective responses to subsequent, unseen challenges. Such evaluations should measure task success, adaptation costs, verification effort, and the recurrence of previously resolved failures. Table 8 links observed failures to candidate optimization targets and the work-agent capabilities discussed in Section 2.3. 

Table 8 Choosing an optimization target from observed failures. The comparisons are proposed tests; the cited studies provide methods for the corresponding changes.    

> Failure evidence Candidate change How to test the update
> An error recurs with the required evidence visible Train interpretation or action patterns [169, 188] Same harness; held-out objects and requests testing the recurring decision Evidence is absent, truncated, or difficult to retrieve Revise observations and retrieval [3, 225] Same model; evidence recovery, task success, and context cost A dependency is lost after interruption or source revision Revise workflows and external state storage [231, 234] Resume from saved state; check unfinished dependencies and affected artifacts again An artifact marked complete fails an independent requirement check Revise requirement capture and verification [167, 175] Independent requirement checks; missed defects, false rejection, and assessment effort Replay diagnoses incompatible context, task, harness, or verifier components Repair the training environment [168] Check that the repaired task runs, preserves requirements, and supports policy transfer to other environments

Together with the continuation protocol, these comparisons suggest evaluating work agents across successive changes in tasks, tools, requirements, and shared workspaces. Targeted failures can test specific improvements, while held-out workflows and later business tasks can assess their transfer. Beyond immediate task success, evaluations should track preserved dependencies, necessary rechecks, human intervention, and the time and cost of accepted work. For RSI, repeated evaluations should further determine whether improvements to the optimization process help agents acquire capabilities needed for increasingly unfamiliar work. This distinction provides a basis for studying both sustained adaptation and longer-term capability growth. 

## 5 Evaluation and Resources: Assessing Work Agents 

Rigorous evaluation is essential for understanding the capabilities and limitations of current models on work tasks and guiding the improvement of work agents. By verifying whether delegated work meets explicit acceptance criteria, evaluation reveals where agents succeed, where they fail, and which capabilities require further development. These findings provide empirical feedback for the improvement methods discussed in Section 4. To serve this purpose, evaluation must clearly define the delegated work, specify its acceptance criteria, and collect appropriate evidence of completion. Answers, files, application state, and complete 39 workflows have different requirements, so assessment must examine the relevant deliverables, workspace state, and execution process rather than rely on the final response alone. Because measured performance depends jointly on the model, tool interface, execution environment, and verification procedure, benchmark scores must be interpreted alongside the tasks and protocols that produced them. This section covers task construction and domain coverage (Section 5.1), the targets, dimensions, and methods used to evaluate work results (Section 5.2), and the metrics used to summarize those judgments (Section 5.3). It also introduces the execution frameworks and evaluation infrastructure that support task execution and verification (Section 5.4). The final part examines the relationships among task performance, execution cost, and economic value, including how human oversight affects deployment returns (Section 5.5). 

5.1 Benchmarks and Task Construction 

A work-agent benchmark is scoped by two choices: how its tasks are constructed and which work domains they cover [275, 276]. Task construction determines how requests, materials, and execution conditions are produced [277, 278], and thus how realistic and how controllable the resulting tasks are. Domain coverage determines the professional knowledge, work objects, and acceptance requirements involved, and thus how representative the benchmark is of the work agents are asked to perform. 

5.1.1 Task Construction Strategies 

Existing benchmarks mainly use four approaches: expert design, conversion of real requests, construction in interactive environments, and construction from professional workspaces. Each emphasizes a different task source and verification condition; Table 9 lists representative instances. 

Expert-designed and curated tasks. Researchers or domain experts design tasks around knowledge, reasoning, and operational requirements, and supply reference answers, labels, or explicit acceptance criteria. GAIA, FinQA, and LegalBench cover information-seeking reasoning, financial computation, and legal judgment respectively [21, 40, 101]; CADBench (BlenderLLM) refines modeling instructions into check items such as attributes and spatial relations [85]. This approach makes difficulty and requirements easy to control, but it does not necessarily include every interaction condition of a real delegation. 

Tasks built from real requests and existing task collections. These methods extract tasks from real problems, user needs, or existing professional data. SWE-bench uses GitHub issues and their associated code repositories, and SpreadsheetBench curates workbook requests from user forums [16, 56]; CADBench (MIT) instead builds program-reconstruction tasks from existing CAD data [86]. The advantage is that real work objects are preserved, although task coverage depends on the data source and the filtering procedure. 

Tasks built around applications and execution environments. These methods place tasks in interactive software environments, constructing repeatably executable tasks by initializing application state, providing operation interfaces, and setting acceptance conditions. WebArena, OSWorld, AndroidWorld, and AppWorld provide web, desktop, mobile, and multi-application API environments respectively, and verify task results against the actual state after execution [6, 7, 27, 37]. Such methods can assess an agent together with the operations it actually performed, but task coverage remains limited by the environment design and by the states that can be verified. 

Tasks built from professional activities and workspaces. These methods organize multi-step, multi-object tasks around occupational requests and actual delivery requirements. GDPval supplies professional work tasks authored by occupational experts, TheAgentCompany uses simulated enterprise services, and Workspace-Bench concerns multiple files and their dependencies [31, 36, 279]; StartupBench and BankerToolBench further emphasize complete professional delivery and fine-grained acceptance [280, 281]. Compared with a single operation, these tasks come closer to a complex delegation, but their construction and verification costs are usually higher. Taken together, the four approaches trade controllability for realism. Expert-designed tasks specify require-ments most precisely, whereas workspace tasks reproduce the most of an actual delegation. In between, real requests contribute authentic content and environments contribute executable context. 40 Table 9 Task construction approaches, sources, and input–output forms in representative benchmarks and an associated agent system.

5.1.2 Domain Coverage and Work Requirements 

Task sources determine how tasks are built, not which work they cover. Domain coverage matters because the evidence needed to accept a completed task depends on the work object. In finance, law, and healthcare, the object is mainly an answer or a professional judgment, and acceptance rests on factual grounding, professional rules, and result correctness [21, 101, 116]. In office and document work, it is the delivered file, whose content, native structure, and computational or visual effects must be checked [56, 112]. In customer service and enterprise operations, it is the state of business systems, subject to permissions and operational constraints [139]. Software engineering, data science, and scientific research usually produce executable or reproducible results, verifiable through tests, database queries, predictive outcomes, or scientific conclusions [10, 16, 154, 286, 287]. CAD and 3D graphics additionally require program validity, geometric fidelity, and rendering quality to hold together [85, 86, 130]. Web, desktop, mobile, and API settings, by contrast, are interaction settings reusable across domains; they assess the environment state after an operation rather than professional knowledge, and are therefore listed as a separate group [6, 7, 27, 37]. Cross-domain benchmarks combine several work objects and acceptance requirements: GDPval examines occupational work products, Remote Labor Index authentic remote tasks, and Workspace-Bench and Startup-Bench complex workspace delivery [36, 279, 280, 288]. Table 10 and Figure 8 summarize the work objects and requirements of each group. Together, they illustrate the range from localized question answering to complete professional tasks. Figure 8 groups benchmarks by their primary work domain or setting and summarizes representative knowledge requirements. 

5.2 Evaluation Methodologies 

Evaluating a work agent involves three decisions: which result to inspect, which properties to judge it on, and how to form the judgment. We refer to these as the evaluation target, the evaluation dimensions, and the evaluation method. Targets are the actual answers, artifacts, or environment states; dimensions include correctness, completeness, structure, visual quality, and constraint compliance. Section 5.2.1 covers targets and dimensions, Section 5.2.2 covers methods, and Section 5.3 discusses how judgments are aggregated into scores. 

5.2.1 Evaluation Targets and Evaluation Dimensions 

By the result inspected at final acceptance, benchmarks fall into four levels: answer, artifact, environment state, and workflow. A task may yield several kinds of evidence; its level is set by the object on which final acceptance is decided. 

Answer-level evaluation. The result is text, a number, a label, or a structured response, checked for correctness, completeness, and consistency with reference information. ConvFinQA executes the model’s reasoning program for a chain of interdependent questions about a financial report and compares the result with the gold answer [98]; MMLongBench-Doc asks questions over PDFs averaging 47.5 pages, some of which the document cannot answer [299]. CRMArena has agents query a Salesforce organization but scores only the 42 Finance & accounting

43 submitted answer [148]. Answer-level evaluation suits well-defined outputs, but a correct answer does not establish that the operations behind it, such as CRMArena’s queries, were correct. 

Artifact-level evaluation. The result is a deliverable the agent created or modified, such as code, a document, or a model file. ScienceAgentBench runs each generated program and checks that it executes and that its output meets task-specific success criteria [10]; OmegaUse-OfficeVal requires Office deliverables to be valid, openable, and editable before scoring them against weighted requirements, with penalties for unintended damage [301]. Because each artifact type has its own requirements of use, text similarity cannot replace structural and functional checks. 

Environment-state-level evaluation. The result is the state of a website, application, or business system after execution. Terminal-Bench 2.0 tests the final container state rather than the agent’s commands [122], VisualWebArena checks the final webpage state with model-based checks where visual content matters [284], and WorkBench compares five sandbox databases with a ground-truth outcome while counting mismatching side effects [114]. Checking state rather than actions admits different action paths to the same goal, but the check must also catch unintended modifications and violated constraints. 

Workflow-level evaluation. The result is a multi-step, cross-application, or multi-file delivery, and evaluation covers the final result, intermediate requirements, and dependencies among objects. ToolSandbox matches the trajectory against milestones that must occur and minefields that must not, scoring zero if any minefield is hit [127]. MultiAgentBench uses an LLM-based detector to record which milestones a team reaches and who contributes [309], and Odysseys has an LLM judge assess the full trajectory against each rubric item [310]. This level comes closest to a complete delegation, but it must make clear which process constraints belong to the acceptance conditions. 

5.2.2 Evaluation Methods 

Given the collected evidence, automated benchmarks judge it mainly through rules, LLM judges, or agent judges; occupational experts assess open-ended work products or provide reference judgments. 

Rule-based evaluation. Programs verify the result by answer matching, execution, or assertions over files and state. AutomationBench checks the end state of simulated business applications with deterministic assertions, including changes that must not occur, and gives no partial credit [140]; OfficeBench saves the file system after each task and applies exact, fuzzy, or execution-based checks [76]. Such checks are clear and repeatable but establish only the requirements their tests cover. 

LLM-as-a-Judge. A model judges the result against task requirements, reference material, and a rubric, which suits semantic, professional, and visual qualities that resist formalization [318, 319]. MCP-Atlas has an LLM check each required factual claim in the final answer and passes a task at 0.75 claim coverage [307]; Finance Agent v2 uses a jury of three frontier models [290]; and CI-Work judges, entry by entry, whether an agent’s final action conveys required information or discloses sensitive information [303]. These scores depend on the criteria, the reference evidence, and the judge model, and are not objective fact. 

Agent-as-a-Judge. An evaluating agent gathers evidence itself by reading files, calling tools, or inspecting traces [15]. Workspace-Bench lets its judge inspect output files, inputs, rubrics, and the execution trajectory with shell tools and file parsers [36]; BankerToolBench gives its judge the same tools as the evaluated agent, so it can run code to check spreadsheet formulas [281]; and StartupBench runs a separate judge session per rubric item, which agreed more often with domain experts than a single holistic judge [280]. Agent judges reach evidence scattered across files, but extra tool execution can cause missed checks or unstable judgments. These methods are often combined: WildClawBench pairs deterministic checks and side-effect audits with an LLM or VLM judge for reports and images [311], and CRMArena-Pro scores answers by exact match while a GPT-4o judge assesses confidentiality awareness [302]. For open-ended professional work, expert judgment remains the reference; in Remote Labor Index, trained evaluators and subject experts decide, as a reasonable client would, whether an AI deliverable completes the project at least as well as the human one [288]. 5.3 Evaluation Metrics 

Evaluation methods judge each task; performance metrics aggregate these judgments across tasks or samples. Benchmarks use three kinds: task outcome scores, relative comparison metrics, and weighted performance metrics. All measure result quality or degree of completion; resource metrics such as cost, tokens, and latency are discussed in Section 5.5.1. 

Task outcome scores. These measure whether, or how far, a result meets the acceptance conditions. The most common is the share of successful tasks; in τ -bench, a task succeeds only if the final database matches the ground truth and the agent conveys the required information [29]. Answer-oriented benchmarks use accuracy and matching scores, such as accuracy, F1, or ROUGE-L in LawBench, depending on the task [292]. Rubric scores differ in strictness: GDP.pdf ranks models by a strict pass rate that requires every criterion, alongside a mean rubric score [91]. Some outcomes are continuous, such as year-end total assets after a simulated year of store operation in E-Commerce Bench [33]. Over repeated attempts, pass@ k requires at least one of k

attempts to succeed, and pass k, introduced by τ -bench, requires all of them [29]; APEX-Agents reports both Pass@8 and Pass 8 [157]. Because they differ in unit and strictness, these scores are not directly comparable across benchmarks. 

Relative comparison metrics. Without a unique reference answer, quality can be measured by preferences, win rates, or rankings among candidates. GDPval has occupational experts blindly compare model and professional deliverables, reporting how often the model’s is rated better or as good [279]. CentaurBench turns pairwise judgments from a panel of LLM judges into rankings, separately for automation, where the model produces the deliverable, and augmentation, where it guides a weaker worker model [314]. Such results depend on what is compared and how, and cannot be read apart from the reference set. 

Weighted performance metrics. These aggregate results with explicit weights on criteria or on tasks. PRBench weights each expert criterion with an integer from −10 to 10 , so that undesirable content lowers the score [80]; OmegaUse-OfficeVal weights tasks by recorded human labor time or a task-price proxy [301]. 49 Criterion weights reflect how much each requirement matters and task weights how much each task is worth; both remain performance scores, not measures of economic return.

5.4 Open-Source Harnesses and Execution Environments 

Evaluating a work agent depends not only on the model and the task but also on the harness configuration. An agent harness handles context management, tool invocation, and execution control, while an evaluation harness handles task initialization, run recording, and result verification. OpenHands, SWE-agent, Codex, and Claude Code supply agent execution mechanisms [3, 11, 12, 152]; BrowserGym, the SWE-bench harness, Harbor, and Inspect AI support standardized task execution and evaluation [16, 320–322]. The infrastructure of a benchmark is closely tied to its work objects. Software-engineering tasks depend on code repositories and test environments, web and desktop tasks on resettable interactive environments, 50 and enterprise tasks check business state through APIs and databases, while office and design tasks require verifying native files, computed results, and the actual deliverables. Table 12 summarizes the execution environments and verification mechanisms of representative benchmarks.

Recent work has begun to examine the effect of the harness on evaluation results directly. HAL supports cross-benchmark comparison of different models and agent scaffolds [324]; Harness-Bench further analyzes how model–harness combinations affect task performance and execution behavior [325]. These studies indicate that benchmark scores should be interpreted together with the execution framework, the tool configuration, and the resource budget, rather than attributed entirely to the underlying model. 

5.5 Performance, Cost, and Economic Value 

Task success rates and delivery quality measure what an agent can do, but they do not capture its execution cost or its actual economic value. Recent work extends the evaluation of work agents along three levels: the performance–cost trade-off, the value of the professional work itself, and the utility of deployment. 52 5.5.1 Trading Performance against Execution Cost 

Task performance and execution cost do not always move together. HAL compares task scores and invocation cost jointly, so that a Pareto frontier can identify competitive system configurations under different cost constraints [324]. The analysis applies both to model comparison and to complete agent systems. HarnessTax further finds that when the same model is run under different harnesses, invocation cost can differ substantially even when task success rates are close [326]. Harness-Bench analyzes how the execution configuration affects task performance [325], and automatic adaptation of instructions, tools, and the execution flow improves the cost efficiency of some smaller models [327]. These results show that the performance–cost trade-off depends not only on the choice of model but also on the design of the harness. 

5.5.2 The Value of Professional Work 

Execution cost reflects the resources needed to complete a task, whereas economic value also depends on the task itself. SWE-Lancer connects software-engineering tasks to real market pay [153]; GDPval has occupational experts compare professional deliverables [279]; and Remote Labor Index evaluates end-to-end automation using actual paid remote projects [288]. OmegaUse-OfficeVal further combines office-artifact quality, human completion time, and a task price proxy [301], while a task-completion time horizon uses human task duration to characterize the scale of work an agent can complete [63]. The task distribution of existing agent benchmarks also diverges from the real labor market [328]. Evaluating work value therefore has to account for task representativeness and professional acceptance, and annotation pay or human working hours should not be equated directly with actual economic return. 

5.5.3 Reliable Deployment and Agentic ROI 

High task performance and low invocation cost do not necessarily produce a positive return on deployment. The position that the real barrier to agent usability is agentic ROI argues that an agent’s practical utility depends on delivery quality, the human time saved, and the cost of use, including the effort users spend describing, checking, and revising the work [329]. READY further brings human oversight into reliability and cost evaluation, finding that agents with comparable autonomous accuracy may require different amounts of human assessment to meet the same deployment requirement [330]. Agentic ROI therefore supplies a conceptual view of user utility, and READY an empirical complement in the form of joint human–machine cost under a reliability constraint. Actual deployment return must also account for failure repair, system integration, and long-term maintenance. Overall, the economic evaluation of work agents needs to distinguish the resource cost of executing a task, the potential economic value of the work produced, and the actual deployment return once human oversight is taken into account. The performance–cost Pareto frontier, the harness tax, and agentic ROI complement conventional task-performance evaluation from the perspectives of system efficiency, execution configuration, and user utility respectively. Section 6 develops how these systems can extend from completing assigned tasks to carrying responsibility as workspaces and requirements change. 

## 6 Frontiers: Toward Next-Generation Work Agents 

A manager asks an agent to prepare a report for a recurring business meeting. Completing the first report requires finding the right records, resolving discrepancies, and explaining the results. Taking responsibility for later reports means following revisions, obtaining missing information, and acting before each deadline. The manager may also ask for a recommendation that reflects how a trusted analyst would weigh new evidence. Other teams must confirm inputs, resolve shared assumptions, and approve changes before the report can be used. These requests extend delegation in three directions. Sustaining the manager’s goals motivates personal work agents ; representing the analyst’s judgment motivates human digital twins ; coordinating contributions 53 toward the report motivates organizational work systems . In each case, the agent must keep work moving as information and responsibilities change. Experience from one reporting cycle can improve the next, while changes to the method may affect results that others have already used. We develop this agenda through four questions. Section 6.1 examines what people can delegate in these applications. Section 6.2 identifies the advances in environment, persistence, and verification they require. Section 6.3 asks how execution experience can improve the agent while existing work continues. Section 6.4 asks under which work conditions this delegation is beneficial. Figure 9 maps the connections, and Table 13 summarizes the research questions. Future applications

> Continuing service around a user's goals

> A particular person's judgment

> Coordination across roles
