Continuing service around a user’s goals, representation of a particular person’s judgment, and coordination across organizational roles motivate advances in environment, persistence, and verification. Interacting execution and improvement loops support these responsibilities, while accepted work, value in use, and total effort guide the assessment of beneficial delegation.

6.1 Future Applications: From Assistance to Delegated Work 

The three applications concern different parts of the same reporting process: the manager’s continuing goals, the analyst’s professional judgment, and the shared delivery. Each places different demands on what the agent must remember, decide, and verify. 

6.1.1 Personal Work Agents 

A personal work agent helps a user keep an unfinished goal moving across requests. For the manager, this means retaining the purpose of the meeting, gathering materials as they become available, and following up on missing inputs before the deadline. When new information arrives, the agent must decide whether to advance an agreed task, ask the user for a decision, or wait [331]. Proactive-agent research studies anticipation and intervention timing [332, 333]; VibeLifeBench and PAST-Bench examine continuing assistance and gains from retained experience [34, 334]. Such service begins with helping the user specify the work. A manager may know which decision the report should support without knowing which records or analyses it requires. The agent can clarify the decision, 54 Table 13 Research agenda: what people can delegate, which technical advances support it, how execution and learning interact, and where delegation is beneficial. 

Research direction Open question Evidence of progress 6.1 Future applications : what can people delegate? Personal work agents (§6.1.1) How can an agent advance unfinished goals between requests? Timely progress on agreed goals with less repeated specification and intervention. Human digital twins (§6.1.2) How can an agent reflect a particular person’s judgment in new situations? Agreement with that person’s decisions under comparable information, assessed separately from task quality. Organizational work (§6.1.3) How can roles, decision rights, and dependencies be coordinated toward a joint delivery? Completed handoffs and end-to-end deliveries with less rework caused by coordination failures. 

6.2 Technical frontiers : what makes that delegation possible? Environment (§6.2.1) Which work settings and personal differences must practice represent? Transfer across work settings and correspondence to individual judgments in held-out situations. Persistence (§6.2.2) How should goals, personal judgments, commitments, and feedback guide continuing work? Timely action, retained obligations, and updates consistent with the current basis of delegation. Verification (§6.2.3) What evidence establishes work quality, personal fidelity, and return on delegation separately? Separate measures of deliverable quality, person-specific decision agreement, and benefit relative to cost. 

6.3 Interacting loops : how can work continue while capability improves? Work execution (§6.3.1) How can responsibility survive interruptions and changes of executor? Resumed work that preserves confirmed effects, pending dependencies, and agreements. Capability improvement (§6.3.2) Which experience should change memory, skills, harness, or policy? Gains on independent tasks and work settings from targeted updates. Coupling the loops (§6.3.3) Which update scope and timing yield gains after rework and coordination costs? More accepted and used work per total effort, including repair of affected ongoing work. 

6.4 Research outlook : where does the system expand useful work? Value under openness and persistence (§6.4) Under which discovery and continuity demands does delegation pay off? A wider range of work completed and used with sustainable demands on people and resources. 

identify missing inputs, and turn the agreed goal into tasks for specialist executors. Magentic-One’s progress tracking and AutoGen’s configurable agent conversations provide mechanisms for coordinating those tasks [53, 335]. Retaining the open goals, completed work, and events that warrant further action lets the agent resume across reporting cycles with less repeated explanation from the user. Claude Duet, introduced as an experimental release in October 2026, illustrates this direction in a personal service: it checks connected applications, prepares briefings, and continues delegated tasks while the user is away, notifying the user when work is complete or needs a decision [336]. This makes the interval between requests part of the work: the agent must choose what to advance and when the user’s attention is needed. 

6.1.2 Human Digital Twins for Professional Work 

The manager may need a recommendation that reflects how a particular analyst weighs new evidence. A human digital twin aims to represent that person’s judgment across new situations. Two analysts can draw different conclusions from the same records because they use different assumptions or accept different tradeoffs. Professional competence concerns the quality of the work; personal fidelity concerns whose judgment the system represents. Existing studies address parts of this correspondence. LLM-based twin architectures support context-aware knowledge exchange, agents grounded in interviews and questionnaires model individual behavior, and TwinVoice evaluates fidelity to a person’s communication and persona [337–339]. GRACE extends representation into clinical communication through clinician-informed dialogue scripts and retrieved medical knowledge [340]. Extending this correspondence to professional action requires learning how a particular person weighs evidence, handles uncertainty, and chooses among acceptable courses of action. 55 Such a twin should be evaluated on held-out cases in which the represented person makes or assesses a decision from comparable information. A personalized professional agent that knows the person’s preferences provides a comparison for testing whether the twin also captures characteristic judgments. The twin’s recommendations also enter work done by other participants. Authenticated delegation provides mechanisms for scoped authority [341], while handoff studies show how constraints can be lost when context is transferred [342]. Passing the analysis to the manager’s agent therefore requires retaining both its assumptions and the authority under which it was produced. 

6.1.3 Organizational Work Systems 

Organizational work joins contributions into a delivery that other people can use. The analyst’s forecast must reflect assumptions agreed with other teams, reach the manager’s report, and arrive before the meeting. MetaGPT provides an example of coordination through structured roles, procedures, and intermediate artifacts in software development [54]. TheAgentCompany places agents in shared applications with simulated colleagues [31]. Failure analyses and controlled scaling studies show how communication structure, verification, and task dependencies affect multi-agent execution [343, 344]. The central challenge is coordinating role responsibilities, decision rights, and interdependent contributions toward a joint delivery. A revised sales estimate may require an analyst to update the forecast, another team to confirm an assumption, and a manager to authorize release. The system must determine who is responsible for each unresolved dependency, which decisions can proceed in parallel, and how revisions affect work already passed to another participant. Human–agent teamwork research and WORKBank’s occupational preferences inform how people choose to share those responsibilities [345, 346]. Progress requires dependable handoffs and conflict resolution that advance the overall delivery while limiting avoidable rework. 

6.2 Technical Frontiers for Sustained Delegation 

The three applications place different demands on sustained delegation: personal agents must advance goals between requests, digital twins must remain faithful to a particular person’s judgment, and organizational systems must coordinate work across roles. Environment research asks how to represent situations that exercise these abilities. Persistence asks how goals, personal judgments, commitments, and dependencies remain available and applicable over time. Verification asks which evidence establishes progress on the delegated responsibility, correspondence to the represented person, and the quality and value of the resulting work. 

6.2.1 Environment: Learning across Realistic Work Worlds 

Environment research must decide what work to represent, which features make the experience realistic, how to run it, and where additional practice is most useful. 

Occupational coverage and transfer. A teacher, analyst, and procurement specialist may all produce presentations while relying on different knowledge and standards. O*NET organizes occupational requirements; GDPval and the Remote Labor Index connect professional tasks to deliverables; WORKBank adds preferences about delegation [279, 288, 346, 347]. Together with analyses of occupational exposure [348], these resources can guide coverage across domains, occupations, and workflows. Testing held-out combinations would reveal what transfers: reconciling inconsistent records may reuse a common procedure, while deciding why a discrepancy matters may require knowledge of the new profession. Those gaps then guide the construction of further experience. For digital twins, environments should also vary the represented person within the same occupation and task. The same reporting problem may lead two analysts to different choices because they use different assumptions or commitments. Holding the available evidence and authorization constant where feasible would isolate differences in judgment; varying authorization separately would test whether actions remain within the delegated role. 56 Decision-relevant realism. Within each kind of work, an environment should preserve the relationships that affect decisions. Workspace-Bench makes file dependencies explicit; business and workspace synthesis methods construct connected entities and evidence relations [36, 143, 163]. Generating workspaces through successive episodes of activity could add the histories behind these relationships. Earlier reports would establish conventions, revisions would create competing versions, and parallel workflows would leave plausible but irrelevant materials. Shared-state settings such as τ 2-bench and Collaborative Gym add participants who change the environment [30, 141], while simulation-to-reality studies examine transfer to real interactions [349]. Varying source ambiguity, dependency depth, and incomplete updates would then exercise the decisions needed to clarify and repair work. 

Execution infrastructure. These environments need to support repeated practice and continuation from interrupted work [350]. BrowserGym provides common web observations and actions, while Agent Lightning connects harness execution to policy training [260, 320, 351]. DSec separates stateful rollouts from preemptible GPU training [352], and AIOpsLab composes services, workloads, faults, and telemetry for operational tasks [123]. Building on these mechanisms, a work environment could package the application versions, source materials, business state, and events needed to reproduce an episode, together with checks for its outcome. Saved states and records of completed effects would let training and evaluation resume the same unfinished work. 

Practice allocation. Once environments are available, the remaining question is which experiences deserve the execution budget. Repository environments provide executable practice [353, 354]; Skill2Env targets capability gaps, and AgentGym-RL progressively expands interaction horizons [355, 356]. For professional work, an expensive episode involving clarification and coordination may teach more than many easily scored actions. Comparing learning gains and transfer across these choices would turn the construction methods in Section 4.1 into a curriculum guided by the work agents still struggle to perform. 

6.2.2 Persistence: Memory, Initiative, and Adaptation 

Realistic environments expose changes that unfold between interactions. Handling them requires maintaining an open responsibility: work that remains due even when no agent is currently running. The reporting deadline, pending source confirmation, and authority to release the result must survive the pause. This creates three connected needs: remembering what still applies, deciding when to act, and learning from repeated work. 

Memory grounded in current work. The agent needs to retrieve relevant decisions and procedures and determine whether they still apply. Personalized workflow prediction studies which procedures fit a user’s context [357]. Memory management and temporal representations support retention and revision [234, 358–361]; A-MEM links and revises notes, while Memory-R1 learns memory operations and selection from downstream rewards [362, 363]. StreamMemBench exposes gaps between storing evidence, using it, and reusing feedback [364]. In the report, a convention may expire when the audience changes while the reconciliation procedure remains useful. Memory should also distinguish a user’s goals and preferences from a represented person’s judgments, existing commitments, and authorization. Linking each item to the relevant person, role, source, and period of applicability would help the agent distinguish a preference about presentation from a commitment governing the analysis or the authority needed to release it. 

Initiative guided by responsibilities. Retained obligations give the agent a reason to act between requests. Proactive Agent learns from activity traces and judgments of proposed assistance; Proactivity-Gym studies intervention timing and trust [332, 333]. VibeLifeBench examines needs that become visible through inspecting external state [34], and temporal and asynchronous environments provide settings for choosing when to observe, ask, act, or wait [128, 365, 366]. A revised sales record may require several reports to be updated, while an approaching deadline may warrant asking for a missing input. The agent also needs to know which decisions it has been delegated. Collecting a missing input, recommending a conclusion, approving its release, and accepting a new commitment involve different decision rights. It can advance tasks covered by the current delegation and seek a decision when a new choice or commitment lies outside that scope. 57 Learning from repeated work. Repeated reporting cycles supply experience for improving source selection, analysis, and explanations. Reflection, experience abstraction, skill libraries, and workflow memory retain reusable lessons [60, 367–369]; SkillRL connects skill evolution to policy learning, and PAST-Bench measures gains from retained experience [265, 334]. Feedback can revise the current report, the procedure used for later reports, or the representation of the analyst. A corrected source value repairs the current result; repeated reconciliation errors motivate a reusable check; feedback from the analyst reveals how that person weighs evidence. Retaining which part of the work each correction concerns gives the agent a basis for later adaptation. Work quality and personal fidelity require separate assessment. Section 6.3.3 examines how procedural updates affect work already in progress. 

6.2.3 Verification: Work Quality, Personal Fidelity, and Return on Delegation 

Continuing service raises three evaluation questions: does the delivery meet its requirements, does a digital twin reflect the represented person’s judgment, and does delegation yield a useful return? These questions require separate evidence about work quality, personal fidelity, and benefit relative to human and computational effort. Verification must follow each as the work and its use evolve. 

Task and deliverable acceptance. A report may contain correct numbers yet fail to inform the decision it is meant to support. Professional acceptance therefore combines direct checks with judgments about purpose and audience. PresentBench uses slide-specific checklists, PRBench elicits expert criteria, and GDPval uses expert comparisons of occupational deliverables [17, 80, 279]. Agent-as-a-Judge provides mechanisms for inspecting files and execution evidence against requirements [15]. The research task is to connect these checks to the brief: determine which criteria transfer across occupations, which require expert input, and which requirements need clarification. Variation across web benchmarks and evidence of judge bias make consistency across these settings an important evaluation target [370–373]. 

Personal decision fidelity. A twin’s fidelity is assessed against decisions elicited from the represented person on held-out cases under comparable information. For the same report, two analysts may recommend different forecasts because they weigh uncertainty differently. Both recommendations may meet professional standards, yet only one may reflect the represented person’s judgment. Conversely, a faithful prediction can reproduce that person’s mistake. Evaluation should therefore report decision agreement separately from deliverable quality, record uncertainty or changes in the person’s position, and check execution against the agreed authority. This distinguishes whose judgment the twin represents from the quality of the resulting work. 

Return on delegation. Measuring the effort of delegation requires tracing the whole delivery process. Agentic ROI considers quality relative to a human baseline, time saved after human interaction, and monetary cost [329]. The relevant effort includes specification, supervision, correction, and integration into later work. Computational cost also depends on the harness: HarnessTax reports similar success rates at substantially different API costs for the same model across harnesses on SWE-bench Lite and Terminal-Bench 2.0 [326]. Recording these costs by stage would reveal where changes to the model or harness could reduce the effort of obtaining an accepted result. The benefit side requires following what happens to that result. For the report, useful evidence includes whether it supported discussion at the meeting and reduced the work needed to reach a decision. Such benefits depend on the people and process using the artifact. A natural experiment on Change.org found changes in petition language without improved platform-level outcomes after AI writing assistance became available [374]. Comparisons with alternative ways of completing the same work are therefore needed to estimate the benefit attributable to delegation. These observations complement acceptance checks and supply the evidence used in Section 6.4. 

Evidence over time. New source data may invalidate an accepted analysis, while later use may reveal a missing requirement. StateGuard represents constraints, versioned variables, and conclusions in a validity graph [375]. Linking acceptance checks to the source, artifact, and requirement versions they concern would help identify which results need rechecking. Linking later feedback to the delivery and its use would show 58 which procedures need improvement. These two paths lead directly to the interaction between ongoing execution and learning. 

6.3 Two Interacting Loops: Delivering Work and Improving Capability 

Execution and learning share the same workspace. A new analysis procedure may improve future reports while changing an estimate that another team has already used. Introducing it requires tracing how its effects propagate through intermediate results, downstream decisions, and ongoing commitments. The work execution loop advances current responsibilities and records what happened. The capability improvement loop learns from that experience and changes how later work is done. Their connection supplies the evidence for choosing the scope and timing of updates while work remains active. Figure 10 shows how updates return to ongoing execution. Work execution loop

Figure 10 Work execution and capability improvement during continuing service. A persistent work record retains agreed requirements, source versions, unfinished work, and completed actions. Execution history and feedback from downstream use inform targeted updates. Independent tests assess capability gains, while checks on current work identify affected results and commitments. This evidence guides whether to apply an update, migrate affected work, or retain the current procedure. The benefit of an update depends on its gains relative to the resulting rework and coordination costs. 

6.3.1 The Work Execution Loop 

The execution loop needs a record that lasts across agent runs. A persistent work record can hold the report’s agreed requirements, source versions, pending approvals, and completed actions; case handling provides a precedent for such enduring records [376]. When a revised sales estimate arrives, the agent identifies the affected analysis, updates it, checks the result, and records what remains due. Approval closes that delivery while leaving the next reporting cycle active. Interruptions make the relationship between this record and the workspace especially important. An external action may complete before the agent retains its confirmation [377, 378]. On resuming, the agent needs to inspect the actual effect before deciding to retry or repair, using the execution-state distinctions in Section 2.3.4. Provenance and compensating operations provide mechanisms for reconstructing and correcting these effects [379–382]. The resulting history supports both the next participant taking over the work and the improvement loop diagnosing how it was performed. 59 6.3.2 The Capability Improvement Loop 

The improvement loop uses execution history and feedback from downstream use to identify reusable procedures and changes that could improve later work. If reports repeatedly miss a deadline because the agent discovers inconsistent inputs too late, an earlier source check may be more useful than a stronger drafting model. Other failures may call for professional training, a repaired tool interface, or better timing. The methods in Section 4 provide the update mechanisms; reflection, workflow memory, skill evolution, and harness-aware training offer ways to retain and learn from the experience [60, 260, 265, 369]. The loop then constructs relevant practice, proposes a change, and tests it in independent work settings. For the earlier source check, evaluation would examine whether it catches discrepancies and reduces later rework across different reports. Independent output checks against shared task requirements make gains comparable across changes to both the agent and its grader [383–385]. Environment construction supplies practice that exercises the missing ability; verification tests whether the proposed update improves the resulting work. The remaining question is how to apply a successful update to work already in progress. 

6.3.3 Coupling Learning to Open Responsibilities 

Continual learning studies how updates preserve performance on earlier tasks [386]. During service, other outputs or participants may already depend on results produced by the method being updated. The research problem is to trace the update’s effects through these dependencies. In the reporting example, records should link source versions and analytical assumptions to calculations, conclusions, approvals, and downstream decisions. A new forecasting method may change a budget estimate already used by another team; an additional source check may leave that estimate intact. For a digital twin, these dependencies also include the represented person’s assumptions and subsequent choices. Evaluation therefore follows both task quality and personal fidelity, using the separate assessments in Section 6.2.3. These links define the scope of an update: which results to recompute, which checks to repeat, and which handoffs to reopen. Studies should compare changes to a single check, a reusable procedure, and the full model–harness configuration, measuring how far their effects propagate. This connects capability gains to the repair and coordination needed to put them into use. Audits of self-evolving financial agents further motivate examining how learning changes behavior and its effects [387]. This makes net benefit during service a concrete research target. Studies should vary update scope and timing while tasks remain unfinished, measuring gains in accepted and used work alongside training, inference, rechecking, repair, and human coordination costs over the same period. Comparisons with retaining the current procedure or updating only between deliveries would show when those gains repay the cost of change. The goal is to identify the work conditions and update granularity under which continual improvement pays off, including settings where a stable task template remains the more productive choice. 

6.4 Research Outlook: Useful Work under Openness and Persistence 

The systems above should expand the range of work that people can usefully delegate. Openness and persistence, introduced in Section 2, provide two independent dimensions along which to test that expansion. Preparing a single report in an unfamiliar organization stresses discovery of sources, relationships, and authority. Preparing recurring reports in a fixed environment stresses continuity across revisions and interruptions. Combining the two asks whether an agent can learn its way around changing workspaces while keeping existing work moving. Figure 11 contrasts these settings and the evidence needed to assess them. These conditions create different opportunities and costs. Persistent service can reuse context and procedures across deliveries, but it also requires continued observation, maintenance, and recovery. Open workspaces offer access to tasks whose inputs were never fully specified, while adding discovery and clarification effort. Studies can vary these demands separately and together, using the evidence in Section 6.2.3 and the measures in Section 5.3 over the full period of delegation. The resulting comparisons would show where agents reduce the effort of existing work and where they make previously inaccessible work feasible. 60 Single report 

> Given sources One-off delivery

Single report 

> Unfamiliar workspace One-off delivery

Recurring reports 

> Fixed workflow Continuing responsibility

Recurring reports 

> Changing workspace Continuing responsibility

# Assess each setting 

Accepted work Value in use Total effort   

> Meets agreed requirements Correct · Complete Fit for purpose Observed benefit in use Discussion at the meeting Work needed for a decision Human and computational resources Specification · Supervision Correction · Integration Figure 11 Assessing beneficial delegation across work conditions. The four scenarios illustrate variation in work openness and persistence: single reports from given sources or in an unfamiliar workspace, and recurring reports within a fixed workflow or a changing workspace. Across all four settings, accepted work, value in use, and total human and computational effort provide complementary evidence for assessing delegation.

The division of work between people and agents is part of this comparison. In a product-innovation experiment, individuals with AI assistance matched human teams without AI and produced proposals spanning technical and commercial perspectives [388]. In a study of experienced developers, the tested AI tools increased completion time [389]. Alongside experiments on professional writing [390], these findings motivate testing delegation across tasks and working arrangements. Changes to processes and skills can determine whether local gains improve overall productivity [391, 392]. For the reporting process, faster analysis matters when assessment and integration can turn it into a timely decision. This returns the agenda to the two loops. As execution improves, assessment, integration, or human decisions may become the limiting stage [393]. The execution loop must organize work around that stage, and the improvement loop must address the capability or coordination that limits the next delivery. Progress is a wider range of professional work completed, accepted, and used with sustainable demands on people and resources. 

## 7 Conclusion 

Reliable digital work depends on keeping task requirements, actions, and outcomes connected as work unfolds. The system view developed here makes these connections visible across tasks, domains, and interfaces. As work expands from returning an answer or making a local change to coordinating workflows and related workspace objects, agents must track more dependencies and use the results of one step to guide the next. Openness makes discovery part of this process; persistence carries responsibility across stages and events. The five core capabilities provide a way to understand where the process succeeds and where it breaks down. Work execution supplies observations, procedures, corrections, and feedback that can improve subsequent work. Training can develop reusable knowledge and action patterns, while harness changes can improve access to evidence, execution control, and retained state. Model–harness optimization coordinates these updates as 61 the model and the work change. Evaluation checks whether the resulting system produces answers, artifacts, state changes, and workflow results that meet their requirements. Distinguishing the inspected result, the assessed properties, the judging method, and the reported metric helps identify which part of the work an improvement actually advances. Sustained delegation brings execution and learning together. An agent must respond to new information and improve its methods while completing work under existing agreements. Gains on new tasks must therefore be considered alongside the repair of dependent work and the effort of introducing updates. Personal work agents, human digital twins, and organizational work systems bring different tests of this capability: progress on continuing goals, fidelity to a particular person’s judgment, and coordination toward a joint delivery. Advances in realistic environments, persistent coordination, and verification can widen the conditions under which these systems are useful. Their practical value depends on the work people accept and use, the benefits it brings, and the human and computational effort required to obtain it. 62 References
